3.8
) concretely describe the bijection between
\underline{\Spin}^{c}(Y,K)
and
\underline{\Spin}^{c}(Y_{\lambda},K_{n,\lambda})
. If we take
n=1
, it also recovers
[
HL19
, Corollary 4.5]
.

The spin
c
structures in
\Spin^{c}(W_{\lambda})
that has the same restriction in
\Spin^{c}(Y)
form an orbit with the action given by
\PD[C^{*}]
. Their image under the bijection
E_{Y,\lambda,K}
forms an orbit with the action given by
\PD[\mu]
, whose
A_{Y,K}
values form a
\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
coset. On the other hand, given
\mathfrak{t}\in\Spin^{c}(Y_{\lambda}(K)),
the spin
c
structures in
\Spin^{c}(W_{\lambda})
that restricts to
\mathfrak{t}
form an orbit with the action given by
\PD[C]
. Their image under the bijection
E_{Y,\lambda,K}
forms an orbit with the action given by
\PD[K]
, whose
A_{Y,K}
values have step length
k/d
and
G_{Y,K}
is of period
d
.

3.3.4.
Alexander grading shifts on holomorphic rectangles.

Following Hedden-Levine’s approach, we introduce a function
\mathcal{N}
to help with the computation. Note that the definition is adjusted to reflect the changes we made on the relative periodic domains. For any domain
S
, define

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(S)=-ndn_{w}(S)-(k+md-nd)n_{z}(S)

\displaystyle+(k+md)n_{z_{n}}(S)

(3.19)

\displaystyle-md\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big(n_{z_{j}}(S)-n_{u_{j}}(S)\Big).

Clearly, the definition of
\mathcal{N}
depends on
n
, even though we omit it in the notation.
Similar to the function defined by Hedden and Levine, for any multi-periodic domain
S
(including those with nonzero multiplicity at
z
), we claim
\mathcal{N}(S)=0
. To see this, first observe any domain is a linear combination of
P_{\gamma}
,
Q
,
\Sigma
, thin domains, and
(\alpha,\beta)
periodic domains with
n_{z}=0
. Then one can check that
\mathcal{N}
vanishes for each of these, proving the claim. Note that for different types of domains, the formula for
\mathcal{N}(S)
can be simplified significantly, depending on which basepoints are in the same regions. We record the result as follows (compare with the table in
[
HL19
, Section 5.2]
):

Type of domain

\mathcal{N}(S)

(\alpha,\beta),(\beta,\gamma)
and
(\beta,\delta)

nd(n_{z}(S)-n_{w}(S))

(\alpha,\gamma)

k(n_{z_{n}}(S)-n_{w}(S))

(\alpha,\delta)

(k+md)(n_{z_{n}}(S)-n_{w}(S))

(\gamma,\delta)

-mdn(n_{z_{n}}(S)-n_{u}(S))

(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)

-ndn_{w}(S)-(k-nd)n_{z}(S)+kn_{z_{n}}(S)

(\alpha,\gamma,\delta)

-(k+md)n_{z}(S)+(k+md)n_{z_{n}}(S)-md\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big(n_{z_{j}}(S)-n_{u_{j}}(S)\Big)

(\alpha,\delta,\beta)

-ndn_{w}(S)-(k+md-nd)n_{z}(S)+(k+md)n_{z_{n}}(S)

(\beta,\gamma,\delta)

-ndn_{w}(S)+ndn_{z}(S)-mdn(n_{z_{n}}(S)-n_{u}(S))

Note that on
(\gamma,\delta)
and
(\beta,\gamma,\delta)
domains, due to the absence of
\alpha
curves, all
n_{u_{j}}
takes the same value for
j=1,\cdots,n
, which we simply denote by
n_{u}
in this context, and
n_{z_{j}}=n_{z}
(
=n_{w}
, if further without
\beta
curves). Now we are ready to compute the Alexander grading shifts on rectangles. Compare
[
HL19
, Proposition 5.6]
.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
,
\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}
, and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\delta}
. Assume also
\mathbf{a}
uses
p_{l}
, for some
l=b-m,\cdots,b-1
in the winding region.

(1)

For any
\rho\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\Theta_{\beta\gamma},\Theta_{\gamma\delta},\mathbf{a})
,

(3.20)

\displaystyle n\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})-\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(\rho)-\frac{n(k+md)-n^{2}d}{2}

(3.21)

\displaystyle\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle

\displaystyle=2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})+2dn_{w}(\rho)-2dn_{z}(\rho)+k

(3.22)

\displaystyle\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)),n[R]\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{\nu}\Bigg(2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a})+2(k+md)\Big(n_{z_{n}}(\rho)-n_{z}(\rho)-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad-n(k+md)+n^{2}d\Bigg)

(3.23)

\displaystyle\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)),n[Q]\rangle

\displaystyle=-2m\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)-nm.

(2)

For any
\rho\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{q},\Theta_{\gamma\delta},\Theta_{\delta\beta},\mathbf{x})
,

(3.24)

\displaystyle\tilde{A}(\mathbf{q})-n\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(\rho)+\frac{nk-n^{2}d}{2}

(3.25)

\displaystyle\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)),n[R]\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{\nu}\left(2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{q})-2k\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)-nk+n^{2}d\right)

(3.26)

\displaystyle\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)),[P_{\delta}]\rangle

\displaystyle=2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})+2dn_{z}(\rho)-2dn_{w}(\rho)+(k+md)

(3.27)

\displaystyle\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)),n[Q]\rangle

\displaystyle=2m\left(-n_{z}(\rho)+n_{z_{n}}(\rho)-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)\right)-nm.

(3)

For any
\rho\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{a},\Theta_{\delta\beta},\Theta_{\beta\gamma},\mathbf{q})
,

(3.28)

\displaystyle\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a})-\tilde{A}(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(\rho)-\frac{nmd}{2}

(3.29)

\displaystyle\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)),[P_{n,\delta}]\rangle

\displaystyle=2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a})+2(k+md)\left(n_{z}(\rho)-n_{z_{n}}(\rho)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)\right)+n^{2}d

(3.30)

\displaystyle\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)),[P_{n,\gamma}]\rangle

\displaystyle=2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{q})+2k\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)+n^{2}d

(3.31)

\displaystyle\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)),[Q]\rangle

\displaystyle=2n_{w}(\rho)-2n_{z}(\rho)+m

Proof.

For part (
1
), we consider
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)
rectangles.

For any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
,
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\delta}
, and
\rho\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\Theta_{\beta\gamma},\Theta_{\gamma\delta},\mathbf{a})
, choose
\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}
,
\psi_{1}\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\Theta_{\beta\gamma},\mathbf{q})
, and
\psi_{2}\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{q},\Theta_{\gamma\delta},\mathbf{a})
such that
\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\psi_{1})=\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)|_{X_{\alpha\beta\gamma}}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\psi_{2})=\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)|_{X_{\alpha\gamma\delta}}
. Moreover, by adding copies of
\Sigma
, which does not change the spin
c
structure condition, we may assume that
n_{z}(\rho)=n_{z}(\psi_{1})+n_{z}(\psi_{2})
. Hence,
S=\mathcal{D}(\rho)-\mathcal{D}(\psi_{1}*\psi_{2})
is a quadruply periodic domain with
n_{z}(S)=0
. Since the function
\mathcal{N}
vanishes on all periodic domains, we have:

\displaystyle\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})-\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a})

\displaystyle=(\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})-\tilde{A}(\mathbf{q}))+(\tilde{A}(\mathbf{q})-\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a}))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(\psi_{1})-\frac{nk-n^{2}d}{2}+\mathcal{N}(\psi_{2})-\frac{nmd}{2}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(\rho)-\frac{n(k+md)-n^{2}d}{2}

which proves (
3.20
).

Next, we consider the spin
c
evaluations. Up to thin domains, we have
S=xP_{\gamma}+yR
, where the decomposition is chosen considering fact that classes
[P_{\gamma}]
and
[R]
can be represented by disjoint surfaces in
X_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}
, so
[P_{\gamma}]\cdot[R]=0
in the intersection form on
X_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}
. We need to solve for
x
and
y.
Observe
n_{w}(R)=n_{z}(R)=0,
from which we solve

x=\frac{n_{w}(S)}{k}.

Similarly we have
-n_{z_{n}}(P_{\gamma})+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big(n_{z_{j}}(P_{\gamma})-n_{u_{j}}(P_{\gamma})\Big)=0,
so

y=-\frac{\nu}{nk}\left(-n_{z_{n}}(S)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big(n_{z_{j}}(S)-n_{u_{j}}(S)\Big)\right).

Note that
x
and
y
need not be integers. Using (
3.7
), (
3.6
), and (
3.11
), we compute:

\displaystyle\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\psi_{1}*\psi_{2})+\PD[S]),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\psi_{1}*\psi_{2}))+2\PD[S],[P_{\gamma}]\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\psi_{1})),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle+2x[P_{\gamma}]^{2}+2y[R]\cdot[P_{\gamma}]

\displaystyle=2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})+2d(n_{w}(\psi_{1})-n_{z}(\psi_{1}))+k+2dk\cdot\frac{n_{w}(S)}{k}

\displaystyle=2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})+2d(n_{w}(\psi_{1})-n_{z}(\psi_{1}))+k+2d(n_{w}(\rho)-n_{w}(\psi_{1})-n_{w}(\psi_{2}))

\displaystyle=2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})+2d(n_{w}(\rho)-n_{z}(\psi_{1})-n_{w}(\psi_{2}))+k

\displaystyle=2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})+2d(n_{w}(\rho)-n_{z}(\psi_{1})-n_{z}(\psi_{2}))+k

\displaystyle=2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})+2d(n_{w}(\rho)-n_{z}(\rho))+k,

which proves (
3.21
). (Note the similarity with (
3.11
).) Formula (
3.22
) follows from a similar computation using (
3.14
), as shown below.

\displaystyle\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)),n[R]\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\psi_{1}*\psi_{2})+\PD[S]),[R]\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\psi_{2})),n[R]\rangle+2xn[P_{\gamma}]\cdot[R]+2yn[R]^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{\nu}\Bigg(2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a})+2(k+md)\Big(n_{z_{n}}(\psi_{2})-n_{z}(\psi_{2})-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\psi_{2})-n_{u_{j}}(\psi_{2})\right)\Big)-n(k+md)+n^{2}d\Bigg)

\displaystyle-\frac{2m(k+md)}{\nu}\left(-n_{z_{n}}(S)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big(n_{z_{j}}(S)-n_{u_{j}}(S)\Big)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{\nu}\Bigg(2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a})+2(k+md)\Big(n_{z_{n}}(\psi_{2})-n_{z}(\psi_{2})-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\psi_{2})-n_{u_{j}}(\psi_{2})\right)+n_{z_{n}}(S)

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(S)-n_{u_{j}}(S)\right)\Big)-n(k+md)+n^{2}d\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{\nu}\Bigg(2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a})+2(k+md)\Big(n_{z_{n}}(\psi_{2})-n_{z}(\psi_{2})+n_{z_{n}}(\psi_{1})-n_{z}(\psi_{1})+n_{z_{n}}(\rho)-n_{z_{n}}(\psi_{1})

\displaystyle-n_{z_{n}}(\psi_{2})-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)\Big)-n(k+md)+n^{2}d\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{\nu}\Bigg(2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a})+2(k+md)\Big(n_{z_{n}}(\rho)-n_{z}(\rho)-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)\Big)-n(k+md)+n^{2}d\Bigg)

Finally, to prove (
3.23
), we have:

\displaystyle\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)),n[Q]\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\nu}{k}\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)),n[R]\rangle-\frac{m}{k}\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)),n[P_{\gamma}]\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{k}\Bigg(2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a})+2(k+md)\Big(n_{z_{n}}(\rho)-n_{z}(\rho)-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad-n(k+md)+n^{2}d\Bigg)-\frac{m}{k}\left(2n\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})+2nd\left(n_{w}(\rho)-n_{z}(\rho)\right)+nk\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{k}\Bigg(-2\mathcal{N}(\rho)+2(k+md)\Big(n_{z_{n}}(\rho)-n_{z}(\rho)-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad-2nd\left(n_{w}(\rho)-n_{z}(\rho)\right)-nk\Bigg)

\displaystyle=-2m\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)-nm

as required. This concludes the proof for part (
1
).

For part (
2
), namely the case about
(\alpha,\gamma,\delta,\beta)
rectangles, consider surfaces inside
X_{\alpha\gamma\delta\beta}
. We use the decomposition
S=xR+yP_{\delta},
where

x=\frac{\nu}{n(k+md)}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(S)-n_{u_{j}}(S)\right)\quad\text{and}\quad y=\frac{n_{w}(S)}{k+md}.\quad\qquad\qquad

Similarly, part (
3
) is the case about
(\alpha,\delta,\beta,\gamma)
rectangles, where surfaces are inside
X_{\alpha\delta\beta\gamma}
. We use the decomposition
S=xP_{\delta}+yP_{\gamma},
where

x=\frac{1}{nd}\left(n_{z_{n}}(S)-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(S)-n_{u_{j}}(S)\right)\right)\quad\text{and}\quad y=\frac{1}{nd}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(S)-n_{u_{j}}(S)\right).

The rest of the computations for both of these cases are parallel to part (
1
) and left for the reader.
∎

4.
The filtered large surgery formula

In this section, we prove the filtered large surgery formula for the
(n,1)
–cable of the knot meridian. The proof mostly follows the framework in
[
HL19
, Section 5.5]
, with minor adjustments.

As before, let
K\subset Y
be a knot of order
d>0
in
H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
and
F
a rational surface for
K
. Recall that
W_{\lambda+m\mu}
denotes the cobordism from
Y
to
Y_{\lambda+m\mu}(K)
, induced by
(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\bm{\delta}),
and turning the cobordism around,
W^{\prime}_{\lambda+m\mu}
is a cobordism from
Y_{\lambda+m\mu}(K)
to
Y
, induced by
(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},\bm{\beta}).
For the ease of notation, we write
W_{m}=W_{\lambda+m\mu}
and
W^{\prime}_{m}=W^{\prime}_{\lambda+m\mu}.
We abuse the notation, and will denote by
C
the core of the
2
-handle and
\hat{F}
the capped seifert surface in the cobordism
W^{\prime}_{m}
as well.

Given
\mathfrak{u}\in\Spin^{c}(Y_{\lambda+m\mu}(K))
, let
\mathfrak{v}
denote a spin
c
structure on
W^{\prime}_{m}
that extends
\mathfrak{u}
, we have

\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}+\PD[C]),[\hat{F}]\rangle=\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[\hat{F}]\rangle+2(k+md),

so the values of
\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[\hat{F}]\rangle
taken over all such
\mathfrak{v}
would form a coset in
\mathbb{Z}/2(k+md)
. We recall the following definition from
[
HL19
]
.

Definition 4.1

(Definition 5.7 in
[
HL19
]
)
.

For each
\mathfrak{u}\in\Spin^{c}(Y_{\lambda+m\mu}(K))
, let
\mathfrak{x}_{\mathfrak{u}}
denote the unique spin
c
structure on
W^{\prime}_{m}
extending
\mathfrak{u}
such that

(4.1)

-2(k+md)<\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{x}_{\mathfrak{u}}),[\hat{F}]\rangle\leq 0,

Let
\mathfrak{y}_{\mathfrak{u}}=\mathfrak{x}_{\mathfrak{u}}+\PD[C]
, so that

(4.2)

0<\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{y}_{\mathfrak{u}}),[\hat{F}]\rangle\leq 2(k+md).

and let
\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{u}}=\mathfrak{x}_{\mathfrak{u}}|_{Y}=\mathfrak{y}_{\mathfrak{u}}|_{Y}-\PD[K]
.
Define

(4.3)

\displaystyle s_{\mathfrak{u}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2d}(\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{x}_{\mathfrak{u}}),[\hat{F}]\rangle+k+md)

(4.4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2d}(\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{y}_{\mathfrak{u}}),[\hat{F}]\rangle-k-md),

so that

(4.5)

-\frac{k+md}{2d}<s_{\mathfrak{u}}\leq\frac{k+md}{2d}.

Finally, define

(4.6)

\Delta_{\mathfrak{u}}=\operatorname{\widetilde{gr}}(F^{\infty}_{W^{\prime}_{m},\mathfrak{x}_{u}})=-\frac{(2ds_{\mathfrak{u}}-k-md)^{2}}{4d(k+md)}+\frac{1}{4}.

We define a pair of filtrations
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}},\mathcal{J}_{\mathfrak{u}}
on
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(Y,K,\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{u}})
by the formula

(4.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}([\mathbf{x},i,j])

\displaystyle=\max\{i,j-s_{\mathfrak{u}}\}

(4.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mathfrak{u}}([\mathbf{x},i,j])

\displaystyle=\max\{i-n,j-s_{\mathfrak{u}}\}+\frac{2nd(s_{\mathfrak{u}}-n)+k+md}{2(k+md)}.

The following theorem strengthens the main result of
[
Tru21
]
, as it computes the (absolute) Alexander grading of generators in
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(Y_{\lambda+m\mu}(K),K_{n,\lambda+m\mu})
, not only the filtration levels, and the knot
K
could be in any rational homology sphere, not only in
S^{3}
.
Compare
[
HL19
, Theorem 5.8]
.

Theorem 4.2
.

If
m
is sufficiently large, then for every
\mathfrak{u}\in\Spin^{c}(Y_{\lambda+m\mu}(K))
, there is a doubly-filtered quasi-isomorphism whose grading shift equal to
\Delta_{\mathfrak{u}}

\Lambda^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{u}}\colon\thinspace\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(Y_{\lambda+m\mu}(K),K_{n,\lambda+m\mu},\mathfrak{u})\to\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(Y,K,\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{u}}),

where the latter is equipped with the filtrations
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}
and
\mathcal{J}_{\mathfrak{u}}
, making the diagrams

\textstyle{\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(Y_{\lambda+m\mu}(K),K_{n,\lambda+m\mu},\mathfrak{u})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{u}}}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(Y,K,\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{u}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v^{\infty}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{CF}^{\infty}(Y_{\lambda+m\mu}(K),\mathfrak{u})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F^{\infty}_{W^{\prime}_{m},\mathfrak{x}_{\mathfrak{u}}}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{CF}^{\infty}(Y,\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{u}})}

and

\textstyle{\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(Y_{\lambda+m\mu}(K),K_{n,\lambda+m\mu},\mathfrak{u})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{u}}}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(Y,K,\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{u}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{s},s_{\mathfrak{u}}}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{CF}^{\infty}(Y_{\lambda+m\mu}(K),\mathfrak{u})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F^{\infty}_{W^{\prime}_{m},\mathfrak{y}_{\mathfrak{u}}}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{CF}^{\infty}(Y,\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{u}}+\PD[K])}

commute up to chain homotopy.

We make some remarks about the theorem statements before moving on to the proof.

Remark 4.3
.

First, since the filtered chain homotopy type of the knot Floer complexes is a topological invariant of the pair
(Y,K)
, we drop the basepoints from the statements. Note that even though the definition of the map
\Lambda^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{u}}
(spelled out in the proof later in the section) depends on the basepoints, the map itself does not. Next, observe that the statements here are almost the same as those in
[
HL19
, Theorem 5.8]
, except that
K_{n,\lambda+m\mu}
is in place for
K_{\lambda+m\mu}
. Indeed, disregarding the second filtration, the map
\Lambda^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{u}}
here is identical to the one in
[
HL19
, Theorem 5.8]
. The only difference is that
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(Y_{\lambda+m\mu}(K),K_{n,\lambda+m\mu},\mathfrak{u})
is a refiltering of
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(Y_{\lambda+m\mu}(K),K_{\lambda+m\mu},\mathfrak{u})
, due to the different placement of a second basepoint. Reflecting this change, the
\mathcal{J}_{\mathfrak{u}}
filtration on
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(Y,K,\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{u}})
is adjusted accordingly. Finally, the Maslov grading shift is induced by cobordism maps
F^{\infty}_{W^{\prime}_{m},\mathfrak{x}_{\mathfrak{u}}}
and
F^{\infty}_{W^{\prime}_{m},\mathfrak{y}_{\mathfrak{u}}}
, respectively, independent of the choice of a second basepoint. Therefore the statement about the Maslov grading follows directly from Hedden-Levine’s proof.

The bulk of Theorem
4.2
is proved by Ozsváth and Szabó, we only need to show
\Lambda^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{u}}
preserves the second filtration that is defined above by (
4.8
).

We focus on the Heegaard diagram
(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\bm{\delta}^{m,b},w,z,z_{n})
, see Figure
3
for an example. Recall from the discussion in Section
3.1
that
q
is the unique intersecting point of
\alpha_{g}
and
\beta_{g}
and
p_{l}
for
l=b-m,\cdots,b-1
are the intersecting points of
\alpha_{g}
and
\delta_{g}
, following the orientation of
\delta_{g}
. For
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
and
l\in\{b-m,\dots,b-1\}
, we define
\mathbf{x}_{l}^{m,b}\in\mathfrak{S}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta}^{m,b})
to be the point obtained by replacing
q
with
p_{l}
and taking “nearest points” in thin domains. There is a
small triangle

\psi_{\mathbf{x},l}^{m,b}\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{l}^{m,b},\Theta_{\delta\beta},\mathbf{x})
in the winding region satisfying

\displaystyle(n_{w}(\psi_{\mathbf{x},l}),n_{z}(\psi_{\mathbf{x},l}),n_{z_{n}}(\psi_{\mathbf{x},l}))=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(l,0,0)&l\geq 0\\
(0,-l,0)&-n<l<0\\
(0,-l,-l-n)&l\leq-n.\end{cases}

A key ingredient of Ozsváth and Szabó’s large surgery theorem is the notion that every spin
c
structure can be represented by generators in the winding region. Hedden and Levine defined the following stronger version:

Definition 4.4

(Definition 5.10 in
[
HL19
]
)
.

We say
\mathfrak{u}\in\Spin^{c}(Y_{\lambda+m\mu}(K))
is
strongly supported in the winding region
if

•

every
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\delta}
with
\mathfrak{s}_{w}(\mathbf{a})=\mathfrak{u}
is of the form
\mathbf{x}_{l}^{m,b}
for some
l
;

•

moreover
c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{w}(\psi^{m,b}_{\mathbf{x},l}))=\mathfrak{x}_{\mathfrak{u}}
, or equivalently,
c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\psi^{m,b}_{\mathbf{x},l}))=\mathfrak{y}_{\mathfrak{u}}
.

Recall that
\mathfrak{s}_{w}(\mathbf{a})=\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\mathbf{a})
since in the diagram
(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta})
basepoints
w
and
z
are interchangeable. However,
\mathfrak{s}_{w}(\psi)
and
\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\psi)
differ by
PD[C]
;
\mathfrak{s}_{w}(\mathbf{x})
and
\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\mathbf{x})
differ by
\PD[K]
. Hedden and Levine proved that this improved condition can be satisfied.

Lemma 4.5

(Lemma 5.12 in
[
HL19
]
)
.

There exists an
M
such that for all
m\geq M
, for every
\mathfrak{u}\in\Spin^{c}(Y_{\lambda+m\mu}(K))
, there exists some
b
such that
\mathfrak{u}
is strongly supported in the winding region of
(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\bm{\delta}^{m,b},w,z,z_{n})
. (Note that
b
does depend on the choice of
\mathfrak{u}
.)

As pointed out in
[
HL19
, Lemma 5.11]
, a spin
c
structure
\mathfrak{u}
is strongly supported in the winding region of
(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta})
iff

\mathfrak{S}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},w,\mathfrak{u})=\{\mathbf{x}_{A(\mathbf{x})-s_{\mathfrak{u}}}\mid\mathbf{x}\in\mathfrak{S}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},w,\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{u}})\},

where
\mathfrak{S}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},w,\mathfrak{s})=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}\mid\mathfrak{s}_{w}(\mathbf{x})=\mathfrak{s}\}.
This can be seen from (
3.18
) combined with the definition of
s_{\mathfrak{u}}
by (
4.3
).

Proof of Theorem
4.2
.

When
m
is large enough, for a given choice of
\mathfrak{u}\in\Spin^{c}(Y_{\lambda+m\mu}(K))
, fix some
b
to satisfy Lemma
4.5
.

Define

(4.9)

\Lambda^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{u}}\colon\thinspace\operatorname{CF}^{\infty}(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},w,\mathfrak{u})\to\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},w,z,\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{u}})

by

(4.10)

\Lambda^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{u}}([\mathbf{a},i])=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}\\
\mathfrak{s}_{w}(\mathbf{x})=\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{u}}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{a},\Theta_{\delta\beta},\mathbf{x})\\
\mu(\psi)=0\\
\mathfrak{s}_{w}(\psi)=\mathfrak{x}_{\mathfrak{u}}\end{subarray}}\#\mathcal{M}(\psi)[\mathbf{x},i-n_{w}(\psi),i-n_{z}(\psi)+s_{\mathfrak{u}}].

Precomposing with the identification of
\operatorname{CF}^{\infty}(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},w,\mathfrak{u})
with
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},w,z_{n},\mathfrak{u})
,
\Lambda^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{u}}
can also be seen as defined on
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},w,z_{n},\mathfrak{u})
. We will prove
\Lambda^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{u}}
is filtered with respect to
\mathcal{J}_{\mathfrak{u}}
filtration. The proof for
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}
filtration is easier and left for the reader.

Since
\mathfrak{u}
is strongly supported in the winding region, every element of
\mathfrak{S}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},w,\mathfrak{u})
is of the form
\mathbf{x}_{l}
, where
\mathbf{x}\in\mathfrak{S}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},w,\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{u}})
and
l=A(\mathbf{x})-s_{\mathfrak{u}}
.
As in the proof of Proposition
3.5
, we compute

\displaystyle\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x}_{l})=

\displaystyle\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})+\frac{n(k+md)-n^{2}d}{2}+\begin{cases}-lnd&l\geq 0\\
l(k+md-nd)&-n<l<0\\
-n(k+md)-lnd&l\leq-n.\end{cases}

So with
\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})-lnd=nds_{\mathfrak{u}}
, we have

\displaystyle A_{w,z_{n}}([\mathbf{x}_{l},i])=i+

\displaystyle\frac{nd(2s_{\mathfrak{u}}-n)}{2(k+md)}+\frac{n}{2}+\begin{cases}0&l\geq 0\\
l&-n<l<0\\
-n&l\leq-n.\end{cases}

On the other hand, we first consider the small triangles
\psi_{\mathbf{x},l}\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{l},\Theta_{\delta\beta},\mathbf{x})
in the winding region. According to Ozsváth and Szabó, with respect to an energy filtration,
\psi_{\mathbf{x},l}
correspond to the main part of
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{u}}^{\infty}
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mathfrak{u}}([\mathbf{x},i-n_{w}(\psi_{\mathbf{x},l}),i-n_{z}(\psi_{\mathbf{x},l})+s_{\mathfrak{u}}])=

\displaystyle\text{ max}\{-n_{w}(\psi_{\mathbf{x},l})-n,-n_{z}(\psi_{\mathbf{x},l})\}+i+\frac{nd(2s_{\mathfrak{u}}-n)}{2(k+md)}+\frac{n}{2}

\displaystyle=i+\frac{nd(2s_{\mathfrak{u}}-n)}{2(k+md)}+

\displaystyle\frac{n}{2}+\begin{cases}0&l\geq 0\\
l&-n<l<0\\
-n&l\leq-n.\end{cases}

Therefore the small triangles
\psi_{\mathbf{x},l}
preserves the
\mathcal{J}_{\mathfrak{u}}
filtration. Next, for an arbitrary triangle
\psi\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{l},\Theta_{\delta\beta},\mathbf{x})
,
n_{w}(\psi)\geq n_{w}(\psi_{\mathbf{x},l})
and
n_{z}(\psi)\geq n_{z}(\psi_{\mathbf{x},l})
. Thus
\psi
decreases or preserves
\mathcal{J}_{\mathfrak{u}}
from the above computation.

∎

5.
The surgery exact triangle

In this section, we will construct the surgery exact triangle relating the Floer homologies of
Y
,
Y_{\lambda}(K)
, and
Y_{\lambda+m\mu}(K)
for
m
large. The maps will be defined on the chain level. We start with a brief discussion about the Maslov grading.

For a diagram
(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\bm{\gamma},\bm{\delta}^{b,m},w,z,z_{n})
, it is proved in
[
HL19
, Proposition 5.15]
that for fixed large
m
, and
b
within a small range of
\frac{m}{2}
, the Maslov grading of every generator
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\delta^{m,b}}
has constant lower and upper bound. We will adopt a choice of
m
and
b
satisfying the above condition, and henceforth drop them from the notation. Note that the Maslov grading is independent of the choice of
z_{n}
basepoint, therefore the statements in
[
HL19
]
regarding the Maslov grading remain true in our set up as well, and we will restate them when needed.

5.1.
Construction of the exact sequence

We start by pointing out a slight difference in our set up compared to the construction in
[
HL19
]
. In this section we will define our complexes
\operatorname{CF}^{\circ}(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},z)
,
\operatorname{CF}^{\circ}(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\gamma},z)
and
\operatorname{CF}^{\circ}(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},z)
with the basepoint
z
instead of
w
as in
[
HL19
]
. And accordingly, we define the triangle, rectangle and pentagon counting maps with respect to the reference point
z
instead of
w
. This in turn, coincides with the definition in
[
OS08
,
OS11
]
. The reason why we need to make this modification, loosely speaking, is that we need to capture the information of the windings to the left of the
\beta_{g}
curve in the winding region. Aside from this slight change, the proof largely follows from the one in
[
HL19
, Section 6]
, with the only difference being computational details.

The twisted complex
\underline{\operatorname{CF}}^{\infty}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},z;\Gamma_{m})
is generated over
\Gamma_{m}
by all pairs
[\mathbf{x},i]
as usual, where
\Gamma_{m}
denote the group ring
\mathbb{F}[\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z}]
. The differential is given by

(5.1)

\partial(T^{s}\cdot[\mathbf{x},i])=\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\\
\mu(\phi)=1\end{subarray}}\#\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(\phi)\,T^{s+n_{w}(\phi)-n_{z}(\phi)}[\mathbf{y},i-n_{z}(\phi)].

Comparing to previous definitions, the
T
exponent in
[
OS08
,
OS11
]
was formulated as the intersection number of the holomorphic disk with an extra basepoint on
\beta_{g}
, but this quantity is the same as
n_{w}(\phi)-n_{z}(\phi)
. Comparing to the definition in
[
HL19
]
, we merely switched the reference point from
w
to
z
.

We realize
\Gamma_{m}=\mathbb{F}[\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z}]
as the quotient ring
\mathbb{F}[T]/(T^{m}-1)
, and often view it as a subring of
\mathbb{F}[\mathbb{Q}/m\mathbb{Z}]
. Recall that
\mathbb{F}[\mathbb{Q}/m\mathbb{Z}]
is the ring of rational-exponent polynomials with variable
T
, where the coefficient is in
\mathbb{F}
.

The complex
\underline{\operatorname{CF}}^{\infty}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},z;\Gamma_{m})
is isomorphic to
m
copies of
\operatorname{CF}^{\infty}(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},z)
.
Let

(5.2)

\theta\colon\thinspace\underline{\operatorname{CF}}^{+}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},z;\Gamma_{m})\to\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{s}\in\Spin^{c}(Y)}\operatorname{CF}^{+}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\mathfrak{s},z)\otimes T^{-k/d-A(\mathfrak{s})}\Gamma_{m}

be the trivializing map defined by

(5.3)

\theta(T^{s}[\mathbf{x},i])=[\mathbf{x},i]\otimes T^{s-k/d-A(\mathbf{x})}.

One can check that this gives an isomorphism between the two chain complexes.
2
2

2

This trivializing map differs from the one in
[
HL19
]
by a constant factor. This change amounts to shifting the sequence
s_{l}
(defined in Section
1.2
) by one to the left, such that the terms in the sequence
(\mathfrak{s}_{l},s_{l})
in the mapping cone (see the proof in Section
6
) have the same index. Otherwise the index is off by one, due to the choice of
z
reference point instead of
w
.

For each
\mathfrak{s}\in\Spin^{c}(Y)
, recall that
A(\mathfrak{s})
forms a
\mathbb{Q}/Z
coset. So there are
m
different powers of
T
occurring in
\operatorname{CF}^{+}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\mathfrak{s},z)\otimes T^{-k/d-A(\mathfrak{s})}\Gamma_{m}
, with exponents in
\mathbb{Q}/m\mathbb{Z}
. We regularly need to lift these exponents to
\mathbb{Q}
, by choosing the
m
values of
r
satisfying

(5.4)

r\equiv-\frac{k}{d}-A_{Y,K}(\mathfrak{s})\pmod{\mathbb{Z}}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{-k-md}{2d}\leq r<\frac{-k+md}{2d}.

Define chain maps

(5.5)

\displaystyle f_{0}^{+}\colon\thinspace

\displaystyle\underline{\operatorname{CF}}^{+}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},z;\Gamma_{m})\to\operatorname{CF}^{+}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\gamma},z)

(5.6)

\displaystyle f_{1}^{+}\colon\thinspace

\displaystyle\operatorname{CF}^{+}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\gamma},z)\to\operatorname{CF}^{+}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},z)

(5.7)

\displaystyle f_{2}^{+}\colon\thinspace

\displaystyle\operatorname{CF}^{+}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},z)\to\underline{\operatorname{CF}}^{+}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},z;\Gamma_{m})

by the following formulas:

(5.8)

\displaystyle f_{0}^{+}(T^{s}\cdot[\mathbf{x},i])

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\Theta_{\beta\gamma},\mathbf{q})\\
\mu(\psi)=0\\
\mathclap{s+n_{w}(\psi)-n_{z}(\psi)\equiv 0\pmod{m}}\end{subarray}}\#\mathcal{M}(\psi)\,[\mathbf{q},i-n_{z}(\psi)]

(5.9)

\displaystyle f_{1}^{+}([\mathbf{q},i])

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\delta}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{q},\Theta_{\gamma\delta},\mathbf{a})\\
\mu(\psi)=0\end{subarray}}\#\mathcal{M}(\psi)\,[\mathbf{a},i-n_{z}(\psi)]

(5.10)

\displaystyle f_{2}^{+}([\mathbf{a},i])

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{a},\Theta_{\delta\beta},\mathbf{x})\\
\mu(\psi)=0\end{subarray}}\#\mathcal{M}(\psi)\,T^{n_{w}(\psi)-n_{z}(\psi)}\cdot[\mathbf{x},i-n_{z}(\psi)].

Let
f_{0}^{t}
,
f_{1}^{t}
,
f_{2}^{t}
denote the analogous maps on
\operatorname{CF}^{t}
.

Following
[
HM18
]
, the quadrilateral-counting maps

(5.11)

\displaystyle h_{0}^{+}\colon\thinspace

\displaystyle\underline{\operatorname{CF}}^{+}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},z;\Gamma_{m})\to\operatorname{CF}^{+}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},z)

(5.12)

\displaystyle h_{1}^{+}\colon\thinspace

\displaystyle\operatorname{CF}^{+}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\gamma},z)\to\underline{\operatorname{CF}}^{+}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},z;\Gamma_{m})

(5.13)

\displaystyle h_{2}^{+}\colon\thinspace

\displaystyle\operatorname{CF}^{+}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},z)\to\operatorname{CF}^{+}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\gamma},z)

are defined by the following formulas:

(5.14)

\displaystyle h_{0}^{+}(T^{s}\cdot[\mathbf{x},i])

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\delta}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\Theta_{\beta\gamma},\Theta_{\gamma\delta},\mathbf{a})\\
\mu(\rho)=-1\\
\mathclap{s+n_{w}(\rho)-n_{z}(\rho)\equiv 0\pmod{m}}\end{subarray}}\#\mathcal{M}(\rho)\,[\mathbf{a},i-n_{z}(\rho)]

(5.15)

\displaystyle h_{1}^{+}([\mathbf{q},i])

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{q},\Theta_{\gamma\delta},\Theta_{\delta\beta},\mathbf{x})\\
\mu(\rho)=-1\end{subarray}}\#\mathcal{M}(\rho)\,T^{n_{w}(\rho)-n_{z}(\rho)}\cdot[\mathbf{x},i-n_{z}(\rho)]

(5.16)

\displaystyle h_{2}^{+}([\mathbf{a},i])

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{a},\Theta_{\delta\beta},\Theta_{\beta\gamma},\mathbf{q})\\
\mu(\rho)=-1\\
n_{w}(\rho)\equiv n_{z}(\rho)\pmod{m}\end{subarray}}\#\mathcal{M}(\rho)\,[\mathbf{q},i-n_{z}(\rho)]

A standard argument shows that for each
j\in\mathbb{Z}/3
, the following holds. (The second statement relies on pentagon-counting maps, which we will discuss in Section
5.4
.)

•

h_{j}^{+}
is a null-homotopy of
f^{+}_{j+1}\circ f^{+}_{j}
;

•

h_{j+1}^{+}\circ f_{j}^{+}+f_{j+2}^{+}\circ h_{j}^{+}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Therefore, the exact triangle detection lemma
[
OS05
, Lemma 4.2]
implies an exact sequence on homology. Using the same formulas, one can also define the maps on
\operatorname{CF}^{t}
,
\mathbf{CF}^{-}
, and
\mathbf{CF}^{\infty}
.

Each of the three complexes come with an
\mathcal{I}
filtration, and the maps
f_{j}
and
h_{j}
respect the
\mathcal{I}
filtration by definition. We define a second filtration on each complex as follows.(Compare
[
HL19
, Definition 6.1]
.)

Definition 5.1
.

•

The filtration
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\gamma}
on
\operatorname{CF}^{+}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\gamma},z)
is simply the Alexander filtration:

(5.17)

\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\gamma}([\mathbf{q},i])=A_{w,z_{n}}(\mathbf{q})+i.

•

The filtration
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\delta}
on
\operatorname{CF}^{+}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},z)
is the Alexander filtration shifted by a constant on each spin
c
summand. For any spin
c
structure
\mathfrak{u}
, and for any generator
\mathbf{a}
with
\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\mathbf{a})=\mathfrak{u}
, define

(5.18)

\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\delta}([\mathbf{a},i])=A_{w,z_{n}}(\mathbf{a})+i+\frac{nd^{2}m(2s_{\mathfrak{u}}-n)}{2k(k+md)},

where
s_{\mathfrak{u}}
is the number from Definition
4.1
.

•

The filtration
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\beta}
on the twisted complex
\underline{\operatorname{CF}}^{+}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},z;\Gamma_{m})
is defined via the trivialization map
\theta
. For any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
with
\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\mathbf{x})=\mathfrak{s}
, and any
r
that satisfies (
5.4
), define

(5.19)

\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\beta}([\mathbf{x},i]\otimes T^{r})=i-\frac{2ndr+nk+n^{2}d}{2k}.

Namely,
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\beta}
is the trivial filtration shifted by a constant
which depends linearly on the exponent of
T
, and it does not depend on
\mathbf{x}
except via its associated spin
c
structure. We transport this back to
\underline{\operatorname{CF}}^{+}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},z;\Gamma_{m})
via the identification
\theta
.

As is discussed in
[
HL19
]
, the maps
f^{+}_{j}
are not filtered, due to the fact that the filtration shift of each map is a function of spin
c
evaluation. In order to get round this problem, note that it suffices to consider the maps
f^{t}_{j}
to prove quasi-isomorphism. Recall that if we let
X_{*}
denote one of the
4
-manifolds
X_{\alpha\beta\gamma},X_{\alpha\gamma\delta}
and
X_{\alpha\delta\beta}
defined in Section
3.2
(either with three or four subscripts),
\Spin^{c}_{0}(X_{*})
denotes the set of spin
c
structures that restricts to the canonical spin
c
structure represented by the generator
\Theta_{\beta\gamma}
on
Y_{\beta\gamma}
,
\Theta_{\gamma\delta}
on
Y_{\gamma\delta}
and
\Theta_{\delta\beta}
on
Y_{\delta\beta}
, for whichever applicable. Each map
f^{t}_{j}
decomposes over spin
c
structures as

f_{j}^{t}=\sum_{\mathfrak{v}\in\Spin^{c}_{0}(X_{*})}f^{t}_{j,\mathfrak{v}},

where
X_{*}
is the
4
-manifold corresponding to the cobordism and
f^{t}_{j,\mathfrak{v}}
counts only the triangles with
\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\psi)=\mathfrak{v}
. The Maslov grading shift of each
f^{t}_{j,\mathfrak{v}}
is given by a quadratic function of
c_{1}(\mathfrak{v})
, whereas for a fixed
t
, the grading of each generator in
\operatorname{CF}^{t}
is bounded. As a result, only finitely many terms
f^{t}_{j,\mathfrak{v}}
may be nonzero, allowing us to have control over the
\mathcal{J}
filtration shift within this range. We will prove the following proposition. Compare
[
HL19
, Proposition 6.2]
.

Proposition 5.2
.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{N}
. For all
m
sufficiently large, the maps
f_{0}^{t}
,
f_{1}^{t}
, and
f_{2}^{t}
are all filtered with respect to the filtrations
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\beta}
,
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\gamma}
, and
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\delta}
. Moreover, for any triangle
\psi
contributing to any of these maps, the filtration shift of the corresponding term equals
n_{z_{n}}(\psi)
.

The similar problem applies to the rectangle-counting maps. Following Hedden-Levine’s argument, we will define truncated versions
\tilde{h}^{t}_{j}
, as a sum of certain terms
h^{t}_{j,\mathfrak{v}}
satisfying specific constraints. In other words, we simply throw away the “bad” terms. The resulting
\tilde{h}^{t}_{j}
will be homotopic equivalent to the original
h^{t}_{j}
maps but behave nicer with respect to the filtration. We will prove the following proposition, parallel to
[
HL19
, Proposition 6.3]
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{N}
. For all
m
sufficiently large, the maps
\tilde{h}^{t}_{0}
,
\tilde{h}^{t}_{1}
, and
\tilde{h}^{t}_{2}
have the following properties:

•

\tilde{h}^{t}_{j}
is a filtered null-homotopy of
f^{t}_{j+1}\circ f^{t}_{j}
.

•

\tilde{h}^{t}_{j+1}\circ f^{t}_{j}+f^{t}_{j+2}\circ\tilde{h}^{t}_{j}
is a filtered quasi-isomorphism.

For the Maslov grading, observe that the underlying cobordisms remain the same, independent of the choice of
z_{n}
basepoint. It then follows from Hedden-Levine’s argument that maps
f_{j}^{t}
and
h^{t}_{j}
are homogeneous and have the appropriate Maslov grading shifts (for the detailed statement see
[
HL19
, Proposition 6.5]
). Combined with Proposition
5.3
and Proposition
5.2
, we have

Theorem 5.4
.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{N}
. For all
m
sufficiently large, the map

\begin{pmatrix}f_{1}^{t}\\
h_{1}^{t}\end{pmatrix}\colon\thinspace\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\gamma},z)\to\Cone(f_{2}^{t})

is a filtered homotopy equivalence that preserves the grading.

5.2.
Triangle maps

We will prove Proposition
5.2
in this section by looking at
f_{0}^{t}
,
f_{1}^{t}
, and
f_{2}^{t}
individually. (Throughout, we will write
f_{j}^{\circ}
when making statements that apply all the flavors of Heegaard Floer homology.) The proof follows the outline in
[
HL19
]
closely.

5.2.1.
The map
f_{0}^{t}

For any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
with
\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\mathbf{x})=\mathfrak{v}|_{Y}
, and any
r\in\mathbb{Q}/m\mathbb{Z}
, we have:

\displaystyle f_{0,\mathfrak{v}}^{\circ}(\theta^{-1}([\mathbf{x},i]\otimes T^{r}))

\displaystyle=f_{0,\mathfrak{v}}^{\circ}(T^{r+A(\mathbf{x})}\cdot[\mathbf{x},i])

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\Theta_{\beta\gamma},\mathbf{q})\\
\mu(\psi)=0\\
\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\psi)=\mathfrak{v}\\
\mathclap{r+k/d+A(\mathbf{x})+n_{w}(\psi)-n_{z}(\psi)\equiv 0\pmod{m}}\end{subarray}}\#\mathcal{M}(\psi)\,[\mathbf{q},i-n_{z}(\psi)].

According to (
3.11
),

\frac{k}{d}+A(\mathbf{x})+n_{w}(\psi)-n_{z}(\psi)=\frac{\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle+k}{2d}.

Thus,
f_{0,\mathfrak{v}}^{\circ}\circ\theta^{-1}
is nonzero only on the summand

\operatorname{CF}(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\mathfrak{s},z)\otimes T^{r}\Gamma_{m},

where
\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{v}|_{Y}
and

r\equiv-\frac{1}{2d}(\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle+k)\pmod{m}.

On this summand, if we neglect the power of
T
, the composition equals the untwisted cobordism map
F^{\circ}_{W_{\lambda}(K),\mathfrak{v}}
. We also lift
r
to
\mathbb{Q}
with the constraint

\frac{-k-md}{2d}\leq r<\frac{-k+md}{2d}.

In
[
HL19
, Lemma 6.7]
it is proved that for fixed
t\in\mathbb{N}
and all large enough
m
, if
f_{0,\mathfrak{v}}^{t}\neq 0
over spin
c
structure
\mathfrak{v},
then for any
\epsilon>0,

\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle\right\rvert<\epsilon md.

In particular, if we take
\epsilon<1,
this implies if
f_{0,\mathfrak{v}}^{\circ}\circ\theta^{-1}
is nonzero, then

(5.20)

\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle=-2dr-k.

Proposition 5.5

(Proposition 6.8 in
[
HL19
]
)
.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{N}
. For all
m
sufficiently large, the map

f_{0}^{t}\colon\thinspace\underline{\operatorname{CF}}^{t}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},z;\Gamma_{m})\to\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\gamma},z)

is filtered with respect to the filtrations
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\beta}
and
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\gamma}
.

Proof.

Assume
m
is large enough, and let
\mathfrak{v}
be a spin
c
structure for which
f^{t}_{0,\mathfrak{v}}\neq 0
, which satisfies (
5.20
).

For any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
and
\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}
with
\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\mathbf{x})=\mathfrak{v}|_{Y_{\alpha\beta}}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\mathbf{q})=\mathfrak{v}|_{Y_{\alpha\gamma}}
, and any triangle
\psi\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\Theta_{\beta\gamma},\mathbf{q})
contributing to
f^{t}_{0,\mathfrak{v}}
, we compute

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\beta}([\mathbf{x},i]

\displaystyle\otimes T^{r})-\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\gamma}([\mathbf{q},i-n_{z}(\psi)])

\displaystyle=-\frac{2ndr+nk+n^{2}d}{2k}-\frac{\tilde{A}(\mathbf{q})}{k}+n_{z}(\psi)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2ndr+nk+n^{2}d}{2k}-\frac{n\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle-2kn_{z_{n}}(\psi)+2kn_{z}(\psi)-n^{2}d}{2k}+n_{z}(\psi)

\displaystyle=n_{z_{n}}(\psi)

\displaystyle\geq 0

as required. The last equality uses (
5.20
), and (
3.12
).
∎

5.2.2.
The map
f_{1}^{t}

The map
f_{1}^{\circ}
decomposes as a sum

(5.21)

f_{1}^{\circ}=\sum_{\mathfrak{v}\in\Spin^{c}_{0}(X_{\alpha\gamma\delta})}f_{1,\mathfrak{v}}^{\circ}.

We need the following lemma from
[
HL19
]
:

Lemma 5.6

(Lemma 6.9 in
[
HL19
]
)
.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{N}
, when
m
is sufficiently large, if
f_{1,\mathfrak{v}}^{\circ}\neq 0,
then

(5.22)

\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[R]\rangle\right\rvert<\frac{m(k+md)}{\nu}.

Moreover, for any
\mathfrak{u}\in\Spin^{c}(Y_{\lambda+m\mu}(K))
, there is at most one nonzero term landing in
\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},\mathfrak{u},z)
, which satisfies

(5.23)

\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[R]\rangle=\frac{2dms_{\mathfrak{u}}}{\nu}.

The second statement follows from the the range of
s_{\mathfrak{u}}
and the fact that
\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[R]\rangle
has step length
2m(k+md)/\nu
. For each
\mathfrak{u}\in\Spin^{c}(Y_{\lambda+m\mu}(K)),
recall that
s_{\mathfrak{u}}
is the number appeared in Definition
4.1
.

Proposition 5.7

(Proposition 6.10 in
[
HL19
]
)
.

For
m
sufficiently large, the map

f_{1}^{t}\colon\thinspace\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\gamma},z)\to\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},z)

is filtered with respect to the filtrations
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\gamma}
and
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\delta}
.

Proof.

Assume
m
is large enough, and suppose
f^{t}_{1,\mathfrak{v}}
is the only nonzero term landing in
\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},\mathfrak{u},z)
, where
\mathfrak{u}=\mathfrak{v}|_{Y_{\alpha\delta}}
. For any
\mathbf{q}
with
\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\mathbf{q})=\mathfrak{v}|_{Y_{\alpha\gamma}}
and
\mathbf{a}
with
\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\mathbf{a})=\mathfrak{u}
, and any triangle
\psi\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{q},\Theta_{\gamma\delta},\mathbf{a})
contributing to
f^{t}_{1,\mathfrak{v}}
, compute

\displaystyle A(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle-A(\mathbf{a})

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{A}(\mathbf{q})}{k}-\frac{\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a})}{k+md}

\displaystyle=\frac{k(\tilde{A}(\mathbf{q})-\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a}))+md\tilde{A}(\mathbf{q})}{k(k+md)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k+md}\left((k+md)n_{z_{n}}(\psi)-(k+md)n_{z}(\psi)-md\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big(n_{z_{j}}(\psi)-n_{u_{j}}(\psi)\Big)-\frac{nmd}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{md}{k(k+md)}\left(\frac{n\nu}{2m}\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[R]\rangle+k\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big(n_{z_{j}}(\psi)-n_{u_{j}}(\psi)\Big)+\frac{nk}{2}-\frac{n^{2}d}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=n_{z_{n}}(\psi)-n_{z}(\psi)-\frac{2nmd}{2(k+md)}+\frac{md}{k(k+md)}\left(nds_{\mathfrak{u}}+\frac{nk}{2}-\frac{n^{2}d}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=n_{z_{n}}(\psi)-n_{z}(\psi)+\frac{nd^{2}m(2s_{\mathfrak{u}}-n)}{2k(k+md)}.

Thus,

\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\gamma}([\mathbf{q},i])-\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\delta}([\mathbf{a},i-n_{z}(\psi)])=n_{z_{n}}(\psi)\geq 0

as required.
∎

5.2.3.
The map
f_{2}^{t}

Let us first examine how the spin
c
decomposition of
f_{2}^{\circ}
interacts with the trivializing map
\theta
. For any
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\delta}
, we have:

\displaystyle\theta\circ f_{2}^{\circ}([\mathbf{a},i])

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{a},\Theta_{\delta\beta},\mathbf{x})\\
\mu(\psi)=0\end{subarray}}\#\mathcal{M}(\psi)\,[\mathbf{x},i-n_{z}(\psi)]\otimes T^{-k/d+n_{w}(\psi)-n_{z}(\psi)-A_{w,z}(\mathbf{x})}.

By (
3.18
), the
T
exponent is equal to

-\frac{k}{d}+n_{w}(\psi)-n_{z}(\psi)-A_{w,z}(\mathbf{x})=-\frac{\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\delta}]\rangle+k-md}{2d}.

Therefore, the term
\theta\circ f_{2,\mathfrak{v}}^{\circ}
lands in the summand

(5.24)

\operatorname{CF}^{\circ}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\mathfrak{v}|_{Y},z)\otimes T^{r},

where
r\in\mathbb{Q}/m\mathbb{Z}
is given by

r\equiv-\frac{\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\delta}]\rangle+k+md}{2d}\pmod{m}

Neglecting the power of
T
, the composition map equals the untwisted cobordism map
F^{\circ}_{W^{\prime}_{m},\mathfrak{v}}
in the first factor. Fixing
t\in\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon>0
, according to
[
HL19
, Lemma 6.11]
, for all
m
sufficiently large, if
f_{2,\mathfrak{v}}^{t}\neq 0
, then

(5.25)

\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\delta}]\rangle\right\rvert<(1+\epsilon)(k+md).

In particular, assuming that
(1+\epsilon)(k+md)<2md
, then the only spin
c
structures that may contribute to
f^{t}_{2}
are those denoted by
\mathfrak{x}_{\mathfrak{u}}
and
\mathfrak{y}_{\mathfrak{u}}
in Definition
4.1
.

Proposition 5.8

(Proposition 6.12 in
[
HL19
]
)
.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{N}
. For all
m
sufficiently large, the map

f_{2}^{t}\colon\thinspace\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},z)\to\underline{\operatorname{CF}}^{t}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},z;\Gamma_{m})

is filtered with respect to the filtrations
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\delta}
and
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\beta}
.

Proof.

Assume
m
is sufficiently large and
(1+\epsilon)(k+md)<2md
. Suppose
\mathfrak{v}
is a spin
c
structure on
W^{\prime}_{m}
for which
f^{t}_{2,\mathfrak{v}}\neq 0
, and let
\mathfrak{u}=\mathfrak{v}|_{Y_{\lambda+m\mu}(K)}
and
\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{v}|_{Y}
. By (
5.25
), we have

-2md<-(1+\epsilon)(k+md)<\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\delta}]\rangle<(1+\epsilon)(k+md)<2md.

Let
r
denote the rational number satisfying

-k-md\leq 2dr<-k+md\quad\text{and}\quad 2dr\equiv-(\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\delta}]\rangle+k+md)\pmod{2md}.

Note that
r
is one of the exponents appearing in (
5.4
). By (
5.24
),
f^{t}_{2,\mathfrak{v}}
lands in
\operatorname{CF}^{\circ}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\mathfrak{v}|_{Y},z)\otimes T^{r}
. At the same time, by (
4.5
) and (
3.18
), the number
s_{\mathfrak{u}}
satisfies:

-k-md<2ds_{\mathfrak{u}}\leq k+md\quad\text{and}\quad 2ds_{\mathfrak{u}}\equiv\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\delta}]\rangle+k+md\pmod{2(k+md)}.

There are two possible cases to consider.

(i)

If
-(1+\epsilon)(k+md)<\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\delta}]\rangle\leq 0
, in this case
\mathfrak{v}=\mathfrak{x}_{\mathfrak{u}},
and the above inequalities and congruences imply that

(5.26)

\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\delta}]\rangle=-2dr-k-md=2ds_{\mathfrak{u}}-k-md.

(ii)

Otherwise if
0<\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\delta}]\rangle<(1+\epsilon)(k+md)
, in this case
\mathfrak{v}=\mathfrak{y}_{\mathfrak{u}},
and we obtain

(5.27)

\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\delta}]\rangle=-2dr-k+md=2ds_{\mathfrak{u}}+k+md.

Suppose
\psi\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{a},\Theta_{\delta\beta},\mathbf{x})
is any triangle that contributes to
f^{t}_{2,\mathfrak{v}}
,
so that
\theta(f^{t}_{2,\mathfrak{v}}([\mathbf{a},i]))
includes the term
[\mathbf{x},i-n_{z}(\psi)]\otimes T^{r}
. We compute:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\delta}([\mathbf{a},i])

\displaystyle-\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\beta}([\mathbf{x},i-n_{z}(\psi)]\otimes T^{r})

\displaystyle=A_{w,z_{n}}(\mathbf{a})+\frac{2ndr+nk+n^{2}d}{2k}+\frac{nd^{2}m(2s_{\mathfrak{u}}-n)}{2k(k+md)}+n_{z}(\psi)

\displaystyle=\frac{n\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\delta}]\rangle-2(k+md)n_{z}(\psi)+2(k+md)n_{z_{n}}(\psi)-n^{2}d}{2(k+md)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{2ndr+nk+n^{2}d}{2k}+\frac{nd^{2}m(2s_{\mathfrak{u}}-n)}{2k(k+md)}+n_{z}(\psi)

\displaystyle=\frac{n\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\delta}]\rangle-n^{2}d}{2(k+md)}+\frac{2ndr+nk+n^{2}d}{2k}+\frac{nd^{2}m(2s_{\mathfrak{u}}-n)}{2k(k+md)}+n_{z_{n}}(\psi).

Depending on case
(i)
or
(ii)
, we will use either (
5.26
) or (
5.27
) to express the terms in first two fractions with
s_{\mathfrak{u}}
, which gives us:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\delta}([\mathbf{a},i])

\displaystyle-\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\beta}([\mathbf{x},i-n_{z}(\psi)]\otimes T^{r})

\displaystyle=\frac{2nds_{\mathfrak{u}}-n^{2}d}{2(k+md)}-\frac{2nds_{\mathfrak{u}}-n^{2}d}{2k}+\frac{nd^{2}m(2s_{\mathfrak{u}}-n)}{2k(k+md)}+n_{z_{n}}(\psi)

\displaystyle=n_{z_{n}}(\psi)\geq 0

as required.
∎

5.3.
Rectangle maps

In this section we analyze rectangle-counting maps. Following Hedden-Levine’s argument, we will introduce the truncated maps
\tilde{h}^{t}_{0}
,
\tilde{h}^{t}_{1}
, and
\tilde{h}^{t}_{2}
, and prove the first part of Proposition
5.3
with these maps. The proof follows completely from the recipe in
[
HL19
]
, replacing numerical values of Alexander filtration and spin
c
evaluation as appropriate. We write down the whole process for the completeness.

5.3.1.
The map
h_{0}^{t}

Similar to the triangle-counting maps,
h^{\circ}_{0}
splits over spin
c
structures
\mathfrak{v}\in\Spin^{c}_{0}(X_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta})
. The composition map
h_{0,\mathfrak{v}}^{\circ}\circ\theta^{-1}
is nonzero only on the summand
\operatorname{CF}^{\circ}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\mathfrak{s},z)\otimes T^{r}\Gamma_{m}
, where
\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{v}|_{Y}
and

r\equiv-\frac{1}{2d}(\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle+k)\pmod{m},

and on this summand
h_{0,\mathfrak{v}}^{\circ}\circ\theta^{-1}
is equal to an untwisted count of rectangles. Recall the general strategy for the rectangle-counting maps is to throw away bad terms, and to prove the remaining terms still constitute a null-homotopy of the triangle-counting maps. The following definition and lemma are due to Hedden and Levine:

Definition 5.9

(Definition 6.14 in
[
HL19
]
)
.

For given
\epsilon>0
, let
\tilde{h}_{0}^{t}
be the sum of all terms
h^{t}_{0,\mathfrak{v}}
corresponding to spin
c
structures
\mathfrak{v}
which either satisfy both

(5.28)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle<\epsilon md

(5.29)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[R]\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle<\frac{m(k+md)}{\nu}

or satisfy

(5.30)

\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[Q]\rangle\right\rvert=\pm m.

Lemma 5.10

(Lemma 6.15 in
[
HL19
]
)
.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon>0
. For all
m
sufficiently large,
\tilde{h}^{t}_{0}
is a null-homotopy of
f^{t}_{1}\circ f^{t}_{0}
.

We require an extra restraint on the spin
c
evaluation. The following lemma is given by an analysis on the absolute grading, which applies to our case as well.

Lemma 5.11

(Lemma 6.13 in
[
HL19
]
)
.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon>0
. For all
m
sufficiently large, suppose
\mathfrak{v}
is a spin
c
structure with
\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[Q]\rangle\right\rvert=m
, and
h^{t}_{0,\mathfrak{v}}\neq 0
, then

\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\delta}]\rangle\right\rvert<(1+\epsilon)(k+md).

With the restricted spin
c
evaluation, the filtration shifts on the truncated map can be much more effectively controlled. The following is parallel to
[
HL19
, Proposition 6.16]
.

Proposition 5.12
.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{N}
and
0<\epsilon<1
. For all
m
sufficiently large, the map
\tilde{h}^{t}_{0}
is filtered with respect to
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\beta}
and
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\delta}
.

Proof.

We will start by looking at the filtration shift of a general term in
h^{t}_{0,\mathfrak{v}},
before specializing to the case of
\tilde{h}^{t}_{0}
.

Suppose
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\delta}
are generators such that
\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\mathbf{x})=\mathfrak{v}|_{Y}=\mathfrak{s}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\mathbf{a})=\mathfrak{v}|_{Y_{\lambda+m\mu}(K)}=\mathfrak{u}
. Similar as before,
h^{t}_{0,\mathfrak{v}}\circ\theta^{-1}
in nonzero only on the summand
[\mathbf{x},i]\otimes T^{r}
, where
r\in\mathbb{Q}
is given by

\frac{-k-md}{2d}\leq r<\frac{-k+md}{2d}\quad\text{and}\quad r\equiv-\frac{1}{2d}(\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle+k)\pmod{m}.

So for some
p\in\mathbb{Z}
, we can write

(5.31)

\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle=-2dr-k+2pmd.

In other words,
p
is the unique integer for which

(5.32)

(2p-1)md<\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle\leq(2p+1)md.

In particular, if
\mathfrak{v}
satisfies (
5.28
), then
p=0
.

On the other hand, consider the number
s_{\mathfrak{u}}
associated with the spin
c
structure
\mathfrak{u}
. By its definition (
4.3
), combined with (
3.14
) and (
3.17
), we have

\displaystyle 2nds_{\mathfrak{u}}

\displaystyle\equiv\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{n,\delta}]\rangle+n(k+md)\pmod{2n(k+md)}

\displaystyle\equiv 2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a})+n^{2}d-n(k+md)\pmod{2n(k+md)}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\nu}{m}\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),n[R]\rangle.\pmod{2n(k+md)}

Hence for some
q\in\mathbb{Z}
, we can write

(5.33)

\frac{\nu}{m}\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),n[R]\rangle=2nds_{\mathfrak{u}}+2nq(k+md),

so that

(5.34)

(2q-1)(k+md)<\frac{\nu}{m}\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[R]\rangle\leq(2q+1)(k+md).

Again, suppose
\mathfrak{v}
satisfies (
5.29
), then
q=0
.

For a rectangle
\rho\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\Theta_{\beta\gamma},\Theta_{\gamma\delta},\mathbf{a})
that contributes to
h^{\circ}_{0,\mathfrak{v}}
, compute:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\delta}([\mathbf{a},

\displaystyle i-n_{z}(\rho)])-\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\beta}([\mathbf{x},i]\otimes T^{r})

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a})}{k+md}-n_{z}(\rho)+\frac{nd^{2}m(2s_{\mathfrak{u}}-n)}{2k(k+md)}+\frac{2ndr+nk+n^{2}d}{2k}

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{\nu}{m}\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),n[R]\rangle+n(k+md)-n^{2}d}{2(k+md)}+\frac{md(\frac{\nu}{m}\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),n[R]\rangle-2nq(k+md))}{2k(k+md)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{-n\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle-nk+2npmd}{2k}-\frac{nd^{2}m}{2k(k+md)}+\frac{nk+n^{2}d}{2k}

\displaystyle\qquad-n_{z_{n}}(\rho)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{2k}\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),\frac{\nu}{m}[R]-[P_{\gamma}]\rangle+\frac{(p-q)nmd}{k}-\frac{n^{2}d}{2k}+\frac{nk+n^{2}d}{2k}

\displaystyle\qquad-n_{z_{n}}(\rho)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2m}\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),n[Q]\rangle+\frac{(p-q)nmd}{k}+\frac{n}{2}-n_{z_{n}}(\rho)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2m}\left(-2m\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)-nm\right)+\frac{(p-q)nmd}{k}+\frac{n}{2}

\displaystyle\qquad-n_{z_{n}}(\rho)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)

\displaystyle=-n_{z_{n}}(\rho)+\frac{(p-q)nmd}{k}

Therefore, in order to show that
\tilde{h}^{t}_{0}
is filtered, we only need to show that
p-q=0
whenever
\mathfrak{v}
satisfies the conditions from Definition
5.9
.

If
\mathfrak{v}
satisfies (
5.28
) and (
5.29
), we immediately deduce that
p=q=0
. This leaves us with only one case, when
\mathfrak{v}
satisfies (
5.30
), namely
\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[Q]\rangle=em
where
e=\pm 1
. By Lemma
5.11
, we may also assume that
\left\lvert c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\delta}]\right\rvert<(1+\epsilon)(k+md)
. Recall that
[P_{\gamma}]=[P_{\delta}]+d[Q]
and
\frac{\nu}{m}[R]=[P_{\delta}]+\frac{k+md}{m}[Q]
. Therefore,

\displaystyle(e-1-\epsilon)md-(1+\epsilon)k<\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle

\displaystyle<(e+1+\epsilon)md+(1+\epsilon)k

\displaystyle(e-1-\epsilon)(k+md)<\frac{\nu}{m}\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[R]\rangle

\displaystyle<(e+1+\epsilon)(k+md).

Comparing with (
5.31
) and (
5.33
) respectively, for sufficiently large
m
, this implies that
p,q\in\{0,e\}
. Also through (
5.32
)and (
5.34
), we have

2(q-p-1)md+(2q-1)k<ke<2(q-p+1)md+(2q+1)k.

It then follows that
p=q
, as required.

∎

5.3.2.
The map
h_{1}^{t}

We recall the definition of the truncated map
h^{t}_{1}
from Hedden-Levine’s argument.

Definition 5.13

(Definition 6.17 in
[
HL19
]
)
.

Let
\tilde{h}^{t}_{1}
denote the sum of all terms
h^{t}_{1,\mathfrak{v}}
for which
\mathfrak{v}
satisfies

(5.35)

\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[Q]\rangle=\pm m.

Similar to the previous case, the truncated map is enough to fulfill the required condition in Proposition
5.3
, as indicated by the following lemma.

Lemma 5.14

(Lemma 6.19 in
[
HL19
]
)
.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{N}
. For all
m
sufficiently large,
\tilde{h}_{1}^{t}
is a null-homotopy of
f_{2}^{t}\circ f_{1}^{t}
.

An extra spin
c
constraint is needed in the proof, given by the Maslov grading bound.

Lemma 5.15
.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{N}
. For all
m
sufficiently large, if
\mathfrak{v}
is any spin
c
structure with
\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[Q]\rangle\right\rvert=m
, and
h^{t}_{1,\mathfrak{v}}\neq 0
, then
\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle\right\rvert<md
.

We are ready to prove the following proposition, parallel to
[
HL19
, Proposition 6.22]
.

Proposition 5.16
.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{N}
. For all
m
sufficiently large, the map
\tilde{h}_{1}^{t}
is filtered with respect to the filtrations
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\gamma}
and
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\beta}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathfrak{v}
is a spin
c
structure for which
h^{t}_{1,\mathfrak{v}}\neq 0
, and assume
\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[Q]\rangle=em
, where
e=\pm 1.
Let
\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
and be generators such that
\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\mathbf{q})=\mathfrak{v}|_{Y_{\lambda}}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\mathbf{x})=\mathfrak{v}|_{Y}
.

Similar as before, note that
\theta\circ h^{\circ}_{1,\mathfrak{v}}
lands in the summand
[\mathbf{x},i]\otimes T^{r}\Gamma_{m}
, where
r\in\mathbb{Q}
is given by

\frac{-k-md}{2d}\leq r<\frac{-k+md}{2d}\quad\text{and}\quad r\equiv-\frac{1}{2d}(\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\delta}]\rangle+k+md)\pmod{m},

so for some
p\in\mathbb{Z}
, we can write

\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\delta}]\rangle=-2dr-k+(2p-1)md,

which implies that

(5.36)

(2p-2)md<\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\delta}]\rangle\leq(2p)md.

By Lemma
5.15
, we may assume that
\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle\right\rvert<md
. Recall that
[P_{\gamma}]=[P_{\delta}]+d[Q]
, and therefore

\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\delta}]\rangle=\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle-emd.

If
e=1
, this implies
p=0
; if
e=-1
, this implies
p=1
. Either way, it holds that

2p-1=-e
.
By (
3.27
), we have

\displaystyle(2p-2)nm

\displaystyle=-\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),n[Q]\rangle-nm

\displaystyle=-2m\left(-n_{z}(\rho)+n_{z_{n}}(\rho)-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)\right)+nm-nm

\displaystyle=2mn_{z}(\rho)-2mn_{z_{n}}(\rho)+2m\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right).

Now, we compute:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\gamma}([\mathbf{q},i])-\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\beta}([\mathbf{x},i-n_{z}(\rho)]\otimes T^{r})

\displaystyle=A_{w,z_{n}}(\mathbf{q})+\frac{2ndr+nk+n^{2}d}{2k}+n_{z}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2k}\left(2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{q})+2ndr+nk+n^{2}d\right)+n_{z}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2k}\left(2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{q})-n\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\delta}]\rangle+(2p-1)nmd+n^{2}d\right)+n_{z}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2k}\left(2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{q})-2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})+2dn_{w}(\rho)-2dn_{z}(\rho)+(2p-1)nmd-n(k+md)+n^{2}d\right)+n_{z}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2k}\Big(2\mathcal{N}(\rho)+2dn_{w}(\rho)-2dn_{z}(\rho)+(2p-2)nmd\Big)+n_{z}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k}\Big(-ndn_{w}(\rho)-(k+md-nd)n_{z}(\rho)+(k+md)n_{z_{n}}(\rho)-md\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+dn_{w}(\rho)-dn_{z}(\rho)+mdn_{z}(\rho)-mdn_{z_{n}}(\rho)+md\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)\Big)+n_{z}(\rho)

\displaystyle=n_{z_{n}}(\rho)\geq 0.

∎

5.3.3.
The map
h_{2}^{t}

Let us start by recalling the definition of the truncated map
\tilde{h}^{t}_{2}
from
[
HL19
]
, which is similar to the previous case.
For any
\mathfrak{v}\in\Spin^{c}_{0}(X_{\alpha\delta\beta\gamma})
, let
\tilde{h}^{t}_{2}
denote the sum of all terms
h^{t}_{2,\mathfrak{v}}
such that

(5.37)

\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[Q]\rangle=\pm m.

As before, the next two lemmas show that
\tilde{h}^{t}_{2}
satisfies the first condition in Proposition
5.3
and give an extra spin
c
evaluation bound that we will use in the proof, respectively.

Lemma 5.17

(Lemma 6.24 in
[
HL19
]
)
.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{N}
. For all
m
sufficiently large,
\tilde{h}_{2}^{t}
is a null-homotopy of
f_{0}^{t}\circ f_{2}^{t}
. ∎

Lemma 5.18

(Lemma 6.25 in
[
HL19
]
)
.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon>0
. For all
m
sufficiently large, if
\mathfrak{v}
is any spin
c
structure with
\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[Q]\rangle\right\rvert=m
(where
e
is an odd integer), and
h^{t}_{1,\mathfrak{v}}\neq 0
, then

(5.38)

\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[R]\rangle\right\rvert<\frac{m(k+md)}{\nu}.

The following proposition is parallel to
[
HL19
, Proposition 6.27]
.

Proposition 5.19
.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{N}
. For all
m
sufficiently large, the map
\tilde{h}_{2}^{t}
is filtered with respect to the filtrations
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\delta}
and
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\gamma}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{T}_{\delta}
and
\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}
be generators such that
\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\mathbf{q})=\mathfrak{v}|_{Y_{\lambda}}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\mathbf{a})=\mathfrak{v}|_{Y_{\lambda+m\mu}}=\mathfrak{u}
, and suppose
\rho\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{a},\Theta_{\delta\beta},\Theta_{\beta\gamma},\mathbf{q})
is a rectangle that contributes to
h^{t}_{2,\mathfrak{v}}([\mathbf{a},i])
. Associated with
\mathfrak{u}
is a
number
s_{\mathfrak{u}}
, which by its definition (
4.3
) satisfies

-(k+md)<2ds_{\mathfrak{u}}\leq k+md\quad\text{and}\quad 2nds_{\mathfrak{u}}\equiv\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{n,\delta}]\rangle+n(k+md)\pmod{2n(k+md)}.

Therefore, for some
q\in\mathbb{Z}
we have

\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)),[P_{n,\delta}]\rangle=2nds_{\mathfrak{u}}+(2q-1)n(k+md),

so that

(2q-2)(k+md)<\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)),[P_{\delta}]\rangle\leq 2q(k+md).

Next we will assume that
\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[Q]\rangle=em
, where
e=\pm 1
, and
h^{t}_{2,\mathfrak{v}}\neq 0
, so that
\mathfrak{v}
satisfies (
5.38
). We have

\displaystyle\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\delta}]\rangle=\frac{\nu}{m}\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[R]\rangle-\frac{k+md}{m}\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[Q]\rangle.

Using (
5.18
), compare the range of both sides of the equation. If
e=1
, this implies
q=0
; if
e=-1
, this implies
q=1
. In either case, it holds that
2q-1=-e
.

Thus

\displaystyle-(2q-1)m

\displaystyle=em=\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)),[Q]\rangle

\displaystyle=2n_{w}(\rho)-2n_{z}(\rho)+m.

We now compute:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\delta}([\mathbf{a},i])-\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\gamma}([\mathbf{q},i-n_{w}(\rho)])

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a})}{k+md}+\frac{nd^{2}m(2s_{\mathfrak{u}}-n)}{2k(k+md)}-\frac{\tilde{A}(\mathbf{q})}{k}+n_{z}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{2k\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a})-2(k+md)\tilde{A}(\mathbf{q})+nd^{2}m(2s_{\mathfrak{u}}-n)}{2k(k+md)}+n_{z}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{2(k+md)(\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a})-\tilde{A}(\mathbf{q}))-2md\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a})+nd^{2}m(2s_{\mathfrak{u}}-n)}{2k(k+md)}+n_{z}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k}\left(\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a})-\tilde{A}(\mathbf{q})\right)+\frac{nd^{2}m(2s_{\mathfrak{u}}-n)}{2k(k+md)}+n_{z}(\rho)-\frac{md\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a})}{2k(k+md)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k}\left(\mathcal{N}(\rho)-\frac{nmd}{2}\right)+\frac{md\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)),[P_{n,\delta}]\rangle-(2q-1)m\cdot nd(k+md)-n^{2}d^{2}m}{2k(k+md)}+n_{z}(\rho)

\displaystyle-\frac{md\left(\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)),[P_{n,\delta}]\rangle-2(k+md)\left(n_{z}(\rho)-n_{z_{n}}(\rho)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)\right)-n^{2}d\right)}{2k(k+md)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k}\left(\mathcal{N}(\rho)-\frac{nmd}{2}\right)+\frac{nd(k+md)(2n_{w}(\rho)-2n_{z}(\rho)+m)}{2k(k+md)}+n_{z}(\rho)

\displaystyle+\frac{2md(k+md)\left(n_{z}(\rho)-n_{z_{n}}(\rho)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)\right)}{2k(k+md)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k}\left(-ndn_{w}(\rho)-(k+md-nd)n_{z}(\rho)+(k+md)n_{z_{n}}(\rho)-md\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{nd\left(n_{w}(\rho)-n_{z}(\rho)\right)+md\left(n_{z}(\rho)-n_{z_{n}}(\rho)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\rho)-n_{u_{j}}(\rho)\right)\right)}{k}+n_{z}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k}\Big(-ndn_{w}(\rho)-(k-nd)n_{z}(\rho)+kn_{z_{n}}(\rho)+nd\left(n_{w}(\rho)-n_{z}(\rho)\right)\Big)+n_{z}(\rho)

\displaystyle=n_{z_{n}}(\rho)

\displaystyle\geq 0.

∎

5.4.
Pentagon maps

In this section we aim to prove the second part of Proposition
5.3
:
\tilde{h}^{t}_{j+1}\circ f^{t}_{j}+f^{t}_{j+2}\circ\tilde{h}^{t}_{j}
(where
j\in\mathbb{Z}/3
) are filtered quasi-isomorphisms. We will only focus on the case of
j=0
, which is the most technically difficult because of the twisted coefficients. The arguments for
j=1
and
j=2
are similar.
Again, our argument here is completely parallel to the argument presented in
[
HL19
, Section 6.4]
, and we will leave out some of the technical details and focus on the adjustment needed due to a different setting. For a more detailed read, see
[
HL19
, Section 6.4]
.

To begin, let
\tilde{\bm{\beta}}=(\tilde{\beta}_{1},\dots,\tilde{\beta}_{g})
denote a small Hamiltonian isotopy of
\bm{\beta}
, such that each
\tilde{\beta}_{i}
meets
\beta_{i}
in a pair of points. We further require that
v
is an extra reference point such that
v
is in the same region of
\Sigma\smallsetminus(\bm{\alpha}\cup\bm{\beta})
as
z
and in the same region of
\Sigma\smallsetminus(\bm{\alpha}\cup\tilde{\bm{\beta}})
as
w
. Finally, let
\Theta_{\beta\tilde{\beta}}\in\mathbb{T}_{\beta}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\tilde{\beta}}
denote the canonical top-dimensional generator.
3
3

3

This is the same setting as depicted in
[
HL19
, Figure 6]
, although in their text description the order of
w
and
z
is switched by mistake.

Define

\tilde{\Psi}^{t}_{0}=\tilde{h}^{t}_{1}\circ f^{t}_{0}+f^{t}_{2}\circ\tilde{h}^{t}_{0}.

The fact that
\tilde{\Psi}^{t}_{0}
is filtered follows immediately from the previous sections, since it is a sum of filtered maps. In order to show it is a quasi-isomorphism, we relate it to the map

(5.39)

\Phi_{0}^{t}\colon\thinspace\underline{\operatorname{CF}}^{t}(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},z;\Gamma_{m})\to\underline{\operatorname{CF}}^{t}(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\tilde{\bm{\beta}},z;\Gamma_{m})

given by

(5.40)

\Phi_{0}^{t}(T^{s}\cdot[\mathbf{x},i])=\sum_{\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\tilde{\beta}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\Theta_{\beta\tilde{\beta}},\tilde{\mathbf{y}})\\
\mu(\psi)=0\end{subarray}}\#\mathcal{M}(\psi)\,T^{s+n_{w}(\psi)-n_{z}(\psi)}\cdot[\tilde{\mathbf{y}},i-n_{z}(\psi)].

By the work of
[
HM18
]
,
\Phi_{0}^{t}
is a chain isomorphism. Moreover, for any
\psi\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\Theta_{\beta\tilde{\beta}},\tilde{\mathbf{y}})
, we have
A(\mathbf{x})-A(\tilde{\mathbf{y}})=n_{z}(\psi)-n_{w}(\psi)
. It follows that
\Phi_{0}^{\circ}
is a filtered isomorphism with respect to
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\beta}
and
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\tilde{\beta}}
.

The aim is then to show
\tilde{\Psi}^{t}_{0}
is filtered homotopy equivalent to
\Phi_{0}^{t}
. The argument hinges on the following pentagon-counting map, defined in
[
HL19
]
:

(5.41)

g_{0}^{\circ}\colon\thinspace\underline{\operatorname{CF}}^{\circ}(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},z;\Gamma_{m})\to\underline{\operatorname{CF}}^{\circ}(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\tilde{\bm{\beta}},z;\Gamma_{m})

given by

(5.42)

g_{0}^{\circ}(T^{s}\cdot[\mathbf{x},i])=\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{{\tilde{\beta}}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\Theta_{\beta\gamma},\Theta_{\gamma\delta},\Theta_{\delta{\tilde{\beta}}},\mathbf{y})\\
\mu(\sigma)=-2\\
\mathclap{s+n_{w}(\sigma)-n_{v}(\sigma)\equiv 0\pmod{m}}\end{subarray}}\#\mathcal{M}(\sigma)\,T^{n_{v}(\sigma)-n_{z}(\sigma)}[\mathbf{y},i-n_{z}(\sigma)].

Hedden and Levine defined a truncated version of
g_{0}^{t}
, again by throwing away bad terms, and they proceed to show the truncated map
\tilde{g}^{t}_{0}
establishes the required filtered homotopy equivalence between
\tilde{\Psi}^{t}_{0}
and
\Phi_{0}^{t}
. This argument works for our case as well, except that the grading shift on
\tilde{g}^{t}_{0}
needs to be recalculated.

Before we discuss the filtration shifts, let us state analogues of the results of Section
3
for pentagons. To begin, let
V
be the
(\beta,\tilde{\beta})
periodic domain with
\partial V=\beta_{g}-\tilde{\beta}_{g}
. In other word,
V
is the thin domain between
\beta_{g}
and
\tilde{\beta}_{g}
. We have
n_{v}(V)=1
, and
n_{w}(V)=n_{z}(V)=n_{z_{j}}(V)=n_{u_{j}}(V)=0
for
j=1,\cdots,n
. Let
\tilde{P}_{\gamma}
,
\tilde{P}_{\delta}
, and
\tilde{Q}
be the analogues of
P_{\gamma}
,
P_{\delta}
, and
Q
with
\beta
circles replaced by
{\tilde{\beta}}
circles: up to thin domains, we have

[\tilde{P}_{\gamma}]=[P_{\gamma}]+k[V],\quad[\tilde{P}_{\delta}]=[P_{\delta}]+(k+md)[V],\quad\text{and}\quad[\tilde{Q}]=[Q]-m[V].

The Heegaard diagram determines a
4
-manifold
X_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta\tilde{\beta}}
, which admits various decompositions into the pieces described in Section
3.2
; for instance, we have

X_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta\tilde{\beta}}=X_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\cup_{Y_{\alpha\delta}}X_{\alpha\delta\tilde{\beta}}=X_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\cup_{Y_{\alpha\gamma}}X_{\alpha\gamma\delta\tilde{\beta}}.

In the intersection pairing form on
H_{2}(X_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta\tilde{\beta}})
, we have

[V]\cdot[V]=[V]\cdot[Q]=[V]\cdot[\tilde{Q}]=0,

and all other intersection numbers can be deduced accordingly. Compare the following with
[
HL19
, Lemma 6.31]
.

Lemma 5.20
.

For any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\tilde{\beta}}
, and
\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\Theta_{\beta\gamma},\Theta_{\gamma\delta},\Theta_{\delta\tilde{\beta}},\tilde{\mathbf{y}})
, we have:

(5.43)

\displaystyle n\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})-n\tilde{A}(\tilde{\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle=-ndn_{w}(\sigma)-(k+md-nd)n_{z}(\sigma)+(k+md)n_{z_{n}}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-md\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\sigma)-n_{u_{j}}(\sigma)\right)

(5.44)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\sigma)),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle

\displaystyle=2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})+2dn_{w}(\sigma)-2dn_{v}(\sigma)+k

(5.45)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\sigma)),[\tilde{P}_{\delta}]\rangle

\displaystyle=2\tilde{A}(\tilde{\mathbf{y}})+2dn_{z}(\sigma)-2dn_{v}(\sigma)+(k+md)

(5.46)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\sigma)),n[Q]\rangle

\displaystyle=-2m\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\sigma)-n_{u_{j}}(\sigma)\right)-nm

(5.47)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\sigma)),n[\tilde{Q}]\rangle

\displaystyle=2m\Big(-n_{z}(\sigma)+n_{z_{n}}(\sigma)-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\sigma)-n_{u_{j}}(\sigma)\right)\Big)-nm

(5.48)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\sigma)),n[V]\rangle

\displaystyle=2n_{z}(\sigma)-2n_{z_{n}}(\sigma)

Proof.

This proof resembles the one for Proposition
3.11
, building upon the calculations made in Propositions
3.5
and
3.11
.

For any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\tilde{\beta}}
, and
\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\Theta_{\beta\gamma},\Theta_{\gamma\delta},\Theta_{\delta\tilde{\beta}},\tilde{\mathbf{y}})
, choose
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\delta}
,
\rho\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\Theta_{\beta\gamma},\Theta_{\gamma\delta},\mathbf{a})
and
\psi\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{a},\Theta_{\delta\tilde{\beta}},\tilde{\mathbf{y}})
such that
\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)=\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\sigma)|_{X_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\psi)=\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\sigma)|_{X_{\alpha\delta{\tilde{\beta}}}}
. Up to adding copies of
\Sigma,
which does not change the spin
c
structure condition, we may assume that
n_{z}(\sigma)=n_{z}(\rho)+n_{z}(\psi)
. Hence,
S=\mathcal{D}(\sigma)-\mathcal{D}(\rho*\psi)
is a quintuply periodic domain with
n_{z}(S)=0
. Since the function
\mathcal{N}
vanishes on all periodic domains, we have:

\displaystyle n\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})-n\tilde{A}(\tilde{\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle=(n\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})-\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a}))+(\tilde{A}(\mathbf{a})-n\tilde{A}(\tilde{\mathbf{y}}))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(\rho)-\frac{n(k+md)-n^{2}d}{2}+\mathcal{N}(\psi)+\frac{n(k+md)-n^{2}d}{2}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(\sigma)

which proves (
5.43
).

Next, up to thin domains, we have
S=xP_{\gamma}+yR
, similar as before, we can solve

x=\frac{n_{w}(S)}{k}.

Using (
3.7
), (
3.6
), and (
3.21
), we compute:

\displaystyle\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\sigma)),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\rho)),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle+2x[P_{\gamma}]^{2}

\displaystyle=2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})+2d(n_{w}(\rho)-n_{z}(\rho))+k+2d(n_{w}(\sigma)-n_{w}(\rho)-n_{w}(\psi))

\displaystyle=2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})+2d(n_{w}(\sigma)-n_{z}(\rho)-n_{w}(\psi))+k

\displaystyle=2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})+2d(n_{w}(\sigma)-n_{v}(\rho)-n_{v}(\psi))+k

\displaystyle=2\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x})+2d(n_{w}(\sigma)-n_{v}(\sigma))+k,

which proves (
5.44
).
The computations for the rest of the items are similar and left for the reader.

∎

Remark 5.21
.

We can adopt the perspective in Remark
3.7
: for a fixed
n
, the relations in Lemma
5.20
hold for all
n^{\prime}
with
1\leq n^{\prime}\leq n
. In particular, taking
n^{\prime}=1,
then (
5.48
) implies that
\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\sigma)),[V]\rangle
is always an even integer, and (
5.47
) implies that
\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\sigma)),[\tilde{Q}]\rangle=em
for some odd integer
e.

Moreover, by varying
n^{\prime}
, we obtain

n_{z}(\sigma)-n_{z_{1}}(\sigma)=n_{z_{j}}(\sigma)-n_{z_{j+1}}(\sigma)
for
j=1,\cdots,n-1.
This is true because the pentagon class
\sigma
has no
(\alpha,\gamma)
or
(\alpha,\delta)
endpoints.

Definition 5.22

(Definition 6.33 in
[
HL19
]
)
.

Fix
0<\epsilon<\frac{1}{3}
. Let
\tilde{g}^{t}_{0}
denote the sum of all terms
g^{t}_{0,\mathfrak{v}}
for which
\mathfrak{v}
satisfies either both

(5.49)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle<\epsilon md

(5.50)

\displaystyle\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[\tilde{Q}]\rangle

\displaystyle=\pm m,

or it satisfies both

(5.51)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[\tilde{P}_{\delta}]\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle<(1+\epsilon)(k+md)

(5.52)

\displaystyle\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[Q]\rangle

\displaystyle=\pm m,

or it satisfies

(5.53)

\displaystyle\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[V]\rangle

\displaystyle=0.

The following lemma puts further constraints on the spin
c
evaluations. It is stated and proved in
[
HL19
]
.

Lemma 5.23

(Lemma 6.32 in
[
HL19
]
)
.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{N}
and
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{\prime}<1
. For all
m
sufficiently large, if
\mathfrak{v}
is any spin
c
structure for which
g^{t}_{0,\mathfrak{v}}\neq 0
, then the following implications hold:

(1)

If
\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle\right\rvert<\epsilon md
and
\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[\tilde{Q}]\rangle=\pm m
, then

(5.54)

\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[\tilde{P}_{\gamma}]\rangle\right\rvert<\epsilon^{\prime}md.

(2)

If
\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[Q]\rangle=\pm m
and
\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[\tilde{P}_{\delta}]\rangle\right\rvert<(1+\epsilon)(k+md)
, then

(5.55)

\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\delta}]\rangle\right\rvert<(1+\epsilon^{\prime})(k+md).

(3)

If
\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[V]\rangle=0
, then

\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[Q]\rangle=\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[\tilde{Q}]\rangle=\pm m.

We are ready to compute the filtration shifts. The following lemma is parallel to
[
HL19
, Lemma 6.34]
.

Lemma 5.24
.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon>0
. For all
m
sufficiently large, the map
\tilde{g}^{t}_{0}
is filtered with respect to the filtrations
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\beta}
and
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\tilde{\beta}}
.

Proof.

For any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
, we have

\displaystyle(\theta_{\alpha\tilde{\beta}}

\displaystyle\circ g^{t}_{0}\circ\theta_{\alpha\beta}^{-1})([\mathbf{x},i]\otimes T^{r})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\tilde{\beta}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\Theta_{\beta\gamma},\Theta_{\gamma\delta},\Theta_{\delta\tilde{\beta}},\tilde{\mathbf{y}})\\
\mu(\sigma)=-2\\
\mathclap{r+k/d+A_{w,z}(\mathbf{x})+n_{z}(\sigma)-n_{v}(\sigma)\equiv 0\pmod{m}}\end{subarray}}\#\mathcal{M}(\sigma)\,[\tilde{\mathbf{y}},i-n_{z}(\sigma)]\otimes T^{-k/d-A_{w,z}(\tilde{\mathbf{y}})+n_{v}(\sigma)-n_{z}(\sigma)}

So as before, the composition
(\theta_{\alpha\tilde{\beta}}\circ g^{t}_{0,\mathfrak{v}}\circ\theta_{\alpha\beta}^{-1})
is only nonzero on the summand
\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\mathfrak{s},z)\otimes T^{r}\Gamma_{m}
and lands in
\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\tilde{\mathfrak{s}},z)\otimes T^{s}\Gamma_{m}
, where
\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{v}|_{Y_{\alpha\beta}}
,
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}=\mathfrak{v}|_{Y_{\alpha\tilde{\beta}}}
and
r,s\in\mathbb{Q}
are given by

(5.56)

\displaystyle\frac{-k-md}{2d}

\displaystyle\leq r<\frac{-k+md}{2d}

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle\equiv-\frac{1}{2d}(\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle+k)\pmod{m}

\displaystyle\frac{-k-md}{2d}

\displaystyle\leq s<\frac{-k+md}{2d}

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\equiv-\frac{1}{2d}(\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[\tilde{P}_{\delta}]\rangle+k+md)\pmod{m}.

Therefore, for some
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}
we can write

\displaystyle\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle

\displaystyle=-2dr-k+2pmd

\displaystyle\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[\tilde{P}_{\delta}]\rangle

\displaystyle=-2ds-k+(2q-1)md,

so that

(5.57)

\displaystyle(2p-1)md

\displaystyle<\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle\leq(2p+1)md

(5.58)

\displaystyle(2q-2)md

\displaystyle<\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[\tilde{P}_{\delta}]\rangle\leq 2qmd.

Next, assume that
\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[\tilde{Q}]\rangle=em
, where
e
is an odd integer. For any
\mathfrak{v}
that appears in the definition of
\tilde{g}^{t}_{0}
