, we now claim

e=2p-2q+1.

Following the same argument in
[
HL19
]
, we will first establish that
\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[V]\rangle
is small compared to
m
. To be precise, we will show
\left\lvert k\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[V]\rangle\right\rvert<2md
in all three cases in Definition
5.22
.

(i)

If
\mathfrak{v}
satisfies (
5.49
) and (
5.50
), hence (
5.54
) by Lemma
5.23
(
1
),where we take
\epsilon^{\prime}=2\epsilon
. Then

\displaystyle\left\lvert k\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[V]\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\gamma}]-[\tilde{P}_{\gamma}]\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[P_{\gamma}]\rangle\right\rvert+\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[\tilde{P}_{\gamma}]\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle<3\epsilon md

\displaystyle<2md

as required.

(ii)

Suppose that
\mathfrak{v}
satisfies (
5.51
) and (
5.52
) and hence (
5.55
) by Lemma
5.23
(
2
), again taking
\epsilon^{\prime}=2\epsilon
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle(k+md)\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[V]\rangle\right\rvert=\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[\tilde{P}_{\delta}]-[P_{\delta}]\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle<(2+3\epsilon)(k+md).

For
m
sufficiently large, we again obtain
\left\lvert k\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[V]\rangle\right\rvert<2md
, as required.

(iii)

If
\mathfrak{v}
satisfies (
5.53
), then the requirement is met immediately.

We proceed by observing since

[P_{\gamma}]-[P_{\delta}]=d[Q],\quad[\tilde{P}_{\delta}]=[P_{\delta}]+(k+md)[V]\quad\text{and}\quad[\tilde{Q}]=[Q]-m[V],

it follows

(5.59)

\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[\tilde{P}_{\delta}]-[P_{\gamma}]+d[\tilde{Q}]\rangle=\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),k[V]\rangle.

Comparing the range of both sides of the equation, using (
5.57
) and (
5.58
), we obtain

(5.60)

2q-2p-3+e<\frac{k\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[V]\rangle}{md}<2q-2p+1+e.

When
\left\lvert\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),[V]\rangle\right\rvert
is sufficiently small, this implies
e=2p-2q+1,
proving the claim.

For any
\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\Theta_{\beta\gamma},\Theta_{\gamma\delta},\Theta_{\delta\tilde{\beta}},\tilde{\mathbf{y}})
contributing to
\tilde{g}^{t}_{0}
, we compute:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\beta}([\mathbf{x},i]\otimes T^{r})

\displaystyle-\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\tilde{\beta}}([\tilde{\mathbf{y}},i-n_{z}(\sigma)]\otimes T^{s})

\displaystyle=i-\frac{2ndr+nk+n^{2}d}{2k}-(i-n_{z}(\sigma))+\frac{2nds+nk+n^{2}d}{2k}

\displaystyle=\frac{nd(s-r)}{k}+n_{z}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),n[P_{\gamma}]-n[\tilde{P}_{\delta}]\rangle+(2q-2p-1)nmd}{2k}+n_{z}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),n[P_{\delta}]+nd[Q]-n[\tilde{P}_{\delta}]\rangle-enmd}{2k}+n_{z}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),-(k+md)n[V]+nd[Q]\rangle-\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),nd[\tilde{Q}]\rangle}{2k}+n_{z}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),-(k+md)n[V]+nmd[V]\rangle}{2k}+n_{z}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{-k\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}),n[V]\rangle}{2k}+n_{z}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=n_{z_{n}}(\sigma)-n_{z}(\sigma)+n_{z}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=n_{z_{n}}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\geq 0.

∎

Lemma 5.25

(Lemma 6.35 in
[
HL19
]
)
.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{N}
. For all
m
sufficiently large, the map
\tilde{g}^{t}_{0}
is a (filtered) chain homotopy between
\tilde{\Psi}^{t}_{0}
and
\Phi^{t}_{0}
.

Proof.

The proof in
[
HL19
]
can proceed without modulation, except that in the last item (P-
5
) where
\sigma
has the decomposition
\sigma=\rho*\psi
, where
\rho\in\pi_{2}(\Theta_{\beta\gamma},\Theta_{\gamma\delta},\Theta_{\delta\tilde{\beta}},\tilde{\Theta})
and
\psi\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\Theta,\tilde{\mathbf{y}})
with
\tilde{\Theta}\in\mathbb{T}_{\beta}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\tilde{\beta}}
, now
z
and
z_{n}
are in the same region of both
(\Sigma,\bm{\beta},\bm{\gamma},\bm{\delta},\bm{\tilde{\beta}})
and
(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\bm{\tilde{\beta}})
. As a result,
n_{z}(\sigma)=n_{z_{n}}(\sigma)
. By (
5.48
),
\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{z}(\sigma)),[V]\rangle=0
, so
\mathfrak{v}
satisfies the condition (
5.53
) and must appear in the definition of
\tilde{g}^{t}_{0}
.

∎

This concludes the proof of Proposition
5.3
, and hence of Theorem
5.4
.

6.
Proof of the filtered mapping cone formula

We are ready to prove the filtered mapping cone formula. According to Lemma
2.1
, it suffices to prove the mapping cone formula for
\operatorname{CF}^{t}
. The proof follows closely the recipe from
[
HL19
, Section 7]
, and we will write down the steps for completeness.

Proof of Theorem
1.9
.

We will only prove the case when
k>0
. Given
t\in\mathbb{N}
, by Theorem
5.4
, for a diagram
(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\bm{\delta},w,z,z_{n})
and large enough
m
, we have doubly-filtered homotopy equivalence

\begin{pmatrix}f_{1}^{t}\\
h_{1}^{t}\end{pmatrix}\colon\thinspace\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\gamma},z)\to\operatorname{Cone}(f_{2}^{t}).

Fixing a spin
c
structure
\mathfrak{t}\in\Spin^{c}(Y_{\lambda}(K))
, consider all its extension in
\Spin^{c}(W_{\lambda}).
As discussed at the end of Section
3.3.3
, these spin
c
structures form an orbit with the action of
\PD[C]
, where
C
denotes the
2
-handle attached to
Y
. Their image under the bijection
E_{Y,\lambda,K}
form an orbit in
\underline{\Spin}^{c}(Y,K)
with the action of
\PD[K];
call them
\xi_{l}
. The Alexander gradings
A_{Y,K}(\xi_{l})
with
l\in\mathbb{Z}
are precisely the arithmetic sequence
s_{l}
with step length
k/d
, as defined in Section
1.2
, where the index is fixed by

\frac{(2l-1)k}{2d}<s_{l}\leq\frac{(2l+1)k}{2d}.

Under the bijection
E_{Y_{\lambda},K_{n,\lambda}}\circ(E_{Y,\lambda,K})^{-1}\colon\thinspace\underline{\Spin}^{c}(Y,K)\to\underline{\Spin}^{c}(Y_{\lambda},K_{n,\lambda})
, the set
\{\xi_{l}\}
is identified with a sequence of spin
c
structures in
\underline{\Spin}^{c}(Y_{\lambda},K_{n,\lambda})
(also with the action of
\PD[K]
). Their
A_{Y_{\lambda},K_{n,\lambda}}
values form a
\mathbb{Q}/n\mathbb{Z}
coset, which we denote by
A_{Y_{\lambda},K_{n,\lambda}}(\mathfrak{t}).
By (
3.10
), we have

nds_{l}\equiv kA_{Y_{\lambda},K_{n,\lambda}}(\mathfrak{t})+\frac{-nk+n^{2}d}{2}\pmod{\mathbb{Z}}

Next, define
\mathfrak{v}_{l}\in\Spin^{c}_{0}(\bar{X}_{\alpha\gamma\delta})
to be the spin
c
structure extending
\mathfrak{t}
with

\frac{(2l-1)mk}{\nu}<\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}_{l}),[R]\rangle\leq\frac{(2l+1)mk}{\nu},

and let
\mathfrak{u}_{l}=\mathfrak{v}_{l}|_{Y_{\alpha\delta}}
. We want to show
s_{\mathfrak{u}_{l}}=s_{l}
. (For the definition of
s_{\mathfrak{u}_{l}}
see (
4.3
). By (
3.15
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{n\nu}{2m}\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}_{l}),[R]\rangle

\displaystyle=\tilde{A}(\mathbf{q})-k\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(n_{z_{j}}(\psi)-n_{u_{j}}(\psi)\right)+\frac{-nk+n^{2}d}{2}

\displaystyle\equiv kA_{Y_{\lambda},K_{n,\lambda}}(\mathfrak{t})+\frac{-nk+n^{2}d}{2}\pmod{k}

\displaystyle\equiv nds_{l}.\pmod{k}

Therefore

\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}_{l}),[R]\rangle=\frac{2mds_{l}}{\nu}.

On the other hand, according to Lemma
5.6
,

\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{v}_{l}),[R]\rangle=\frac{2mds_{\mathfrak{u}_{l}}}{\nu},

which shows
s_{\mathfrak{u}_{l}}=s_{l}
, as required. Lemma
5.6
further shows that
f^{t}_{1,\mathfrak{v}_{l}}
is only non zero when
-L\leq l\leq L
for some
L
. In other words, the image of
f^{t}_{1}
restricted to
\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\gamma},\mathfrak{t})
is contained in the direct sum of
\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},\mathfrak{u}_{l})
for
-L\leq l\leq L
.

Recall that spin
c
structures
\mathfrak{x}_{\mathfrak{u}_{l}},\mathfrak{y}_{\mathfrak{u}_{l}}
on
W_{\alpha\delta\beta}
, both restricting to
\mathfrak{u}_{l}
on
Y_{\alpha\delta}
, are characterized by

\displaystyle\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{x}_{\mathfrak{u}_{l}}),[P_{\delta}]\rangle

\displaystyle=2ds_{l}-k-md

\displaystyle\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{y}_{\mathfrak{u}_{l}}),[P_{\delta}]\rangle

\displaystyle=2ds_{l}+k+md.

We will write
\mathfrak{x}_{l}=\mathfrak{x}_{\mathfrak{u}_{l}}
and
\mathfrak{y}_{l}=\mathfrak{y}_{\mathfrak{u}_{l}}
to simplify the notation. The proof of Proposition
5.8
shows that
\mathfrak{x}_{l}
and
\mathfrak{y}_{l}
are the only two spin
c
structures that may contribute to the map
f^{t}_{2}
on
\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},\mathfrak{u}_{l})
.
Observe
\mathfrak{x}_{l}
and
\mathfrak{y}_{l-1}
restrict to the same spin
c
structure
\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{u}_{l}}
on
Y
. We will write
\mathfrak{s}_{l}=\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{u}_{l}}.

Moreover, the images of the maps
\theta\circ f^{t}_{2,\mathfrak{x}_{l}}
and
\theta\circ f^{t}_{2,\mathfrak{y}_{l-1}}
both lie in the summand
\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\mathfrak{s}_{l})\otimes T^{-s_{l}}
.

Therefore, the complex
\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\gamma},w,\mathfrak{t})
equipped with filtrations
\mathcal{I}_{\alpha\gamma}
and
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\gamma}
, is doubly-filtered quasi-isomorphic to the doubly-filtered complex

(6.1)

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},\mathfrak{u}_{l-1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F^{t}_{W^{\prime}_{m},\mathfrak{x}_{l-1}}}
\scriptstyle{F^{t}_{W^{\prime}_{m},\mathfrak{y}_{l-1}}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},\mathfrak{u}_{l})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F^{t}_{W^{\prime}_{m},\mathfrak{x}_{l}}}
\scriptstyle{F^{t}_{W^{\prime}_{m},\mathfrak{y}_{l}}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\mathfrak{s}_{l-1})\otimes T^{-s_{l-1}}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\mathfrak{s}_{l})\otimes T^{-s_{l}}}
\textstyle{\cdots}

where the filtrations are inherited from those on
\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},z)
and
\underline{\operatorname{CF}}^{t}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},z;\Gamma_{m})
, respectively.

By Theorem
4.2
, there are doubly-filtered quasi-isomorphisms

\Lambda^{t}_{\mathfrak{u}_{l}}\colon\thinspace\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},\mathfrak{u}_{l})\to A^{t}_{\mathfrak{s}_{l},s_{l}}

where the Alexander filtration on
\Lambda^{t}_{\mathfrak{u}_{l}}
is identified with the filtration
\mathcal{J}_{\mathfrak{u}_{l}}
from (
4.8
). Whereas each
\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\mathfrak{s}_{l})\otimes T^{-s_{l}}
can be identified with
B^{t}_{\mathfrak{s}_{l}}=C_{\mathfrak{s}_{l}}\{0\leq i\leq t\}
, so that the complex in (
6.1
) is quasi-isomorphic to

(6.2)

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{t}_{\xi_{l-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v^{t}_{\xi_{l-1}}}
\scriptstyle{h^{t}_{\xi_{l-1}}}
\textstyle{A^{t}_{\xi_{l}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v^{t}_{\xi_{l}}}
\scriptstyle{v^{t}_{\xi_{l}}}
\scriptstyle{h^{t}_{\xi_{l}}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B^{t}_{\mathfrak{s}_{l-1}}}
\textstyle{B^{t}_{\mathfrak{s}_{l}}}
\textstyle{\cdots}

This is the complex
X^{t}_{\lambda,\mathfrak{t},n,-L,L}
from Section
1.2
by definition.

Disregarding the second filtration, the underlying mapping cone is isomorphic to the one defined in
[
HL19
]
, and with the mapping cone in
[
OS11
]
, for this purpose. The maps induced by cobordisms are independent of the choice of basepoints. Thus the absolute grading shift on our mapping cone matches with Hedden-Levine’s. Placing the
z_{n}
basepoint differently amounts to imposing a different
\mathcal{J}
filtration to the mapping cone. To complete the proof, we just need to check the
\mathcal{J}
filtration agrees with the descriptions (
1.6
) and (
1.9
) in Section
1.2
.

•

On each summand
\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\delta},\mathfrak{u}_{l})
,
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\delta}
is defined in (
5.18
) as the Alexander filtration plus
\frac{nd^{2}m(2s_{\mathfrak{u}_{l}}-n)}{2k(k+md)}
, thus
\mathcal{J}
on
A^{t}_{\xi_{l}}
would be
\mathcal{J}_{\mathfrak{u}_{l}}
plus the same shift:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}([\mathbf{x},i,j])

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}_{\mathfrak{u}_{l}}([\mathbf{x},i,j])+\frac{nd^{2}m(2s_{\mathfrak{u}_{l}}-n)}{2k(k+md)}

\displaystyle=\max\{i-n,j-s_{l}\}+\frac{nd(2s_{l}-n)}{2(k+md)}+\frac{1}{2}+\frac{nd^{2}m(2s_{l}-n)}{2k(k+md)}

\displaystyle=\max\{i-n,j-s_{l}\}+\frac{nds_{l}+nk-n^{2}d}{2k}

as required.

•

On each summand
\operatorname{CF}^{t}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\mathfrak{s}_{l})\otimes T^{-s_{l}}
, according to (
5.19
) we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}([\mathbf{x},i,j])

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\beta}([\mathbf{x},i]\otimes T^{-s_{l}})

\displaystyle=i-\frac{2nd(-s_{l})+nk+n^{2}d}{2k}

\displaystyle=i-n+\frac{2nds_{l}+nk-n^{2}d}{2k}

as required.

We have proved that
\operatorname{CFK}^{t}(Y_{\lambda},K_{n,\lambda},\mathfrak{t})
is doubly-filtered quasi-isomorphic to
X^{t}_{\lambda,\mathfrak{t},n,-L,L}
. According to Lemma
2.1
, this implies the doubly-filtered quasi-isomorphism between
\operatorname{CFK}^{-}(Y_{\lambda},K_{n,\lambda},\mathfrak{t})
and
X^{-}_{\lambda,\mathfrak{t},n,-L,L}
. To pass to the infinity version, tensor both complexes by
\mathbb{F}[U,U^{-1}]
, and we conclude that
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(Y_{\lambda},K_{n,\lambda},\mathfrak{t})
is doubly-filtered quasi-isomorphic to
X^{\infty}_{\lambda,\mathfrak{t},n,-L,L}
, as required.

∎

7.
Rational surgery

Following the same approach spelled out in
[
HL19
, Section 8]
, our formula also supports a generalization to the rational surgery. For the simplicity, in this section we only demonstrate the process for
1/p
surgery on a null-homologous knot
K
inside a rational homology sphere
Y
, where
p
is a positive integer. But this can be generalized to the case of arbitrary rational surgeries on any knot
K
in a rational homology sphere without too much trouble.

Let
K_{n,1/p}
denote the knot in
Y_{1/p}(K)
obtained from
(n,1)
–cable of a left-handed meridian of
K
. Note that the left-handed meridian is no longer isotopic to the surgery solid torus as in the case of integral surgery. Following Hedden and Levine’s notation, let
Y^{\prime}
denote
Y\mathbin{\#}-L(p,1)
and
K^{\prime}=K\mathbin{\#}O_{p}
, where
O_{p}\subset-L(p,1)
is obtained from the Hopf link by performing a
-p
surgery on one of the unknot components. Then
Y_{1/p}(K)
is obtained from
Y^{\prime}
via a
2
-handle cobordism along
K^{\prime};
let
W
denote this cobordism.

For
q\in\{0,\cdots,p-1\}
, the complex
\widehat{\operatorname{HFK}}(-L(p,1),O_{p},\mathfrak{u}_{q})
is generated by a single generator in Alexander grading
-\frac{p-2q-1}{2p}
. For any
\mathfrak{s}\in\Spin^{c}(Y)
, let
\mathfrak{s}_{q}=\mathfrak{s}\mathbin{\#}\mathfrak{u}_{q}\in\Spin^{c}(Y\mathbin{\#}-L(p,1))
. The Künneth principle for connected sums (see
[
OS04a
, Theorem 7.1]
) implies that
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(Y^{\prime},K^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{q})
is isomorphic to
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(Y,K)
, with the Alexander grading shifted by
-\frac{p-2q-1}{2p}
. Moreover, all the
\mathfrak{s}_{q}
are cobordant to the same spin
c
structure on
Y_{1/p}(K)
through
W
; denoted this spin
c
structure by
\mathfrak{t}\in\Spin^{c}(Y_{1/p}(K))
.

In order to compute
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(Y_{1/p}(K),K_{n,1/p},\mathfrak{t}),
we apply the filtered mapping cone formula to
(Y^{\prime},K^{\prime})
. Using the notation from Section
1.2
, we take
d=p
and the framing on
K^{\prime}
corresponds to
k=1
. Consider all the spin
c
structures on
W
that extends
\mathfrak{t}
. Through the bijection between
\Spin^{c}(W)
and
\underline{\Spin}^{c}(Y^{\prime},K^{\prime}),
they form a sequence
\xi_{l}\in\underline{\Spin}^{c}(Y^{\prime},K^{\prime})
for
l\in\mathbb{Z}
, where

\frac{2l-1}{2p}<A_{Y^{\prime},K^{\prime}}(\xi_{l})\leq\frac{2l+1}{2p}.

We set
s_{l}=A_{Y^{\prime},K^{\prime}}(\xi_{l})
. Note that each
A_{Y^{\prime},K^{\prime}}(\xi_{l})
satisfies

A_{Y^{\prime},K^{\prime}}(\xi_{l})\equiv\frac{-p+2q+1}{2p}\pmod{\mathbb{Z}},

for some
q=0,1,\cdots,p-1.
So we can write

s_{l}=\frac{-p+2q+1}{2p}+r

for some
r\in\mathbb{Z}.
The above arithmetic constraint is enough to pin down
s_{l}
and
r
, as demonstrated by the following computation from
[
HL19
]
: Observe
2l-1<(2r-1)p+2q+1\leq 2l+1
, so we have
(2r-1)p\leq 2(l-q)<(2r-1)p+2
. There are two cases:

(i)

If
p
is even, we deduce that
2(l-q)=(2r-1)p
, which implies

s_{l}=\frac{2l+1}{2p};

(ii)

if
p
is odd, then
2(l-q)=(2r-1)p+1
, and therefore

s_{l}=\frac{l}{p}.

In both cases we have

r=\left\lfloor\frac{2l+p}{2p}\right\rfloor.

In the mapping cone, the complexes
A^{\infty}_{\xi_{l}}
and
B^{\infty}_{\xi_{l}}
are each copies of
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(Y^{\prime},K^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{q})
by definition. As noted above, each of these complexes is in turn isomorphic to
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(Y,K,\mathfrak{s})
, but with the
j
coordinate shifted by
-\frac{p-2q-1}{2p}
. Now we can directly compute the filtrations on
A^{\infty}_{\xi_{l}}
and
B^{\infty}_{\xi_{l}}
using the formulas (
1.5
), (
1.6
), (
1.8
), and (
1.9
). We collect the results as follows.

On
A^{\infty}_{\xi_{l}}
,

(7.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{t}}([\mathbf{x},i,j])

\displaystyle=\max\{i,j-r\}

(7.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mathfrak{t}}([\mathbf{x},i,j])

\displaystyle=\max\{i-n,j-r\}+nl-\begin{cases}\frac{n}{2}(np-1)\quad p\text{ odd},\\
\frac{n}{2}(np-2)\quad p\text{ even},\\
\end{cases}

On
B^{\infty}_{\xi_{l}}
,

(7.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{t}}([\mathbf{x},i,j])

\displaystyle=i

(7.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mathfrak{t}}([\mathbf{x},i,j])

\displaystyle=i-n+nl-\begin{cases}\frac{n}{2}(np-1)\quad p\text{ odd},\\
\frac{n}{2}(np-2)\quad p\text{ even}.\\
\end{cases}

In particular, the above formula takes the same form as the mapping cone defined by Ozsváth and Szabó, namely each
A_{s}
and
B_{s}
complex appears
p
times in the mapping cone (though in our case the
\mathcal{J}_{t}
filtration of each copy is shifted accordingly).

8.
Examples and applications

In this section, we compute a series of examples that lead us to the proof of Proposition
1.2
and other applications. For the reader’s convenience, we restate the mapping cone formula for any knot
K\subset S^{3}
under the
+1
-surgery, where the
(n,1)
–cable of the meridian is denoted by
K_{n,1}
. Take
k=d=1
, and
s_{l}=s\in\mathbb{Z}
in this case, since the Alexander grading for any relative spin
c
structure of
K
is an integer.

According to the main theorem, the knot floer complex
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(S^{3}_{1}(K),K_{n,1})
is given by
X^{\infty}_{1,n,a,b}(K)
for some
a\ll 0
and
b\gg 0
. Specifically, let us consider the mapping cone
X^{\infty}_{1,n,-g+1,g+n-1}(K)
(we will see this gives suitable values for
a
and
b
later):

(8.1)

\displaystyle\bigoplus^{g+n-1}_{s=-g+1}A^{\infty}_{s}\xrightarrow{v^{\infty}_{s}+h^{\infty}_{s}}\bigoplus^{g+n-1}_{s=-g+2}B^{\infty}_{s},

where each
A^{\infty}_{s}
and
B^{\infty}_{s}
is isomorphic to
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(S^{3},K)
, the map
v^{\infty}_{s}\colon\thinspace A^{\infty}_{s}\to B^{\infty}_{s}
is the identity and
h^{\infty}_{s}\colon\thinspace A^{\infty}_{s}\to B^{\infty}_{s+1}
is the reflection map precomposed with
U^{s}
. The
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
filtrations are given by

For
[\mathbf{x},i,j]\in A^{\infty}_{s}
,

(8.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}([\mathbf{x},i,j])

\displaystyle=\max\{i,j-s\}

(8.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}([\mathbf{x},i,j])

\displaystyle=\max\{i-n,j-s\}+ns-\frac{n(n-1)}{2}

For
[\mathbf{x},i,j]\in B^{\infty}_{s}
,

(8.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}([\mathbf{x},i,j])

\displaystyle=i

(8.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}([\mathbf{x},i,j])

\displaystyle=i-n+ns-\frac{n(n-1)}{2}.

It is straightforward to check that for
s<-g+1
, the map
h^{\infty}_{s}
induces an isomorphism on the homology; for
s>g+n-1,

the map
v^{\infty}_{s}(K)
induces an isomorphism on the homology. Thus the filtered quasi-isomorphism type of
X^{\infty}_{1,n,a,b}(K)
does not depend on
a,b
if
a\leq-g+1
and
b\geq g+n-1
. We will write

X_{n}^{\infty}(K)=X^{\infty}_{1,n,-g+1,g+n-1}(K).

For the purpose of computations, looking at the associated graded complex is usually helpful. We will describe the associated graded complex with
\mathcal{I}=0
, and the associated graded complex with other
\mathcal{I}
values can be obtained from translations. The complex
X_{n}^{\infty}(K)\{i=0\}
is given by

(8.7)

\displaystyle\bigoplus^{g+n-1}_{s=-g+1}A^{\infty}_{s}\{\text{max}(i,j-s)=0\}\xrightarrow{v^{\infty}_{s}+h^{\infty}_{s}}\bigoplus^{g+n-1}_{s=-g+2}B^{\infty}_{s}\{i=0\},

where each
A^{\infty}_{s}\{\text{max}(i,j-s)=0\}
admits exactly
n+1
filtration levels with respect to
\mathcal{J}
, namely
\mathcal{J}=ns+\frac{n(n-1)}{2},1+ns+\frac{n(n-1)}{2},\cdots,n+ns+\frac{n(n-1)}{2},
while
B^{\infty}_{s}\{i=0\}
corresponds to the filtration level
-n+ns+\frac{n(n-1)}{2}.
See Figure
7
.

Figure 7.
A portion of the complex
X_{n}^{\infty}(K)\{i=0\}
, where the vertical bar of each complex is at
i=0
and the horizontal bar of
A_{s}
(resp.
A_{s-1}
) is at
j=s
(resp.
j=s-1
).

The general strategy for computation involves finding a
reduced
basis for
X^{\infty}_{n}(K),
where every term in the differential strictly lowers at least one of the filtrations. This can be achieved through a cancellation process (see for example
[
LOT18
]
) as follows: suppose
\partial x_{i}=y_{i}

+
lower filtration terms, where the double filtration of
y_{i}
is the same as
x_{i}
, then the subcomplex of
X^{\infty}_{n}(K)
generated by all such
\{x_{i},\partial x_{i}\}
is acyclic, and
X^{\infty}_{n}(K)
quotient by this complex is reduced.

In the rest of the section, we wish to shed a light on the richness of examples coming from the new filtered mapping cone formula. But at first we have to go on a detour for some preliminaries of Heegaard Floer concordance invariants. The experts can skip the next subsection.

8.1.
Concordance invariants and other rings

The concordance invariants in our interests are defined using Heegaard Floer homology over other rings. Pointing the reader to the sources, we would not go in depth about the detailed construction. Instead, we will review some results that are useful to us and use an example to explain some of the intuition.

To start, Ian Zemke provided a reinterpretation of Heegaard Floer homology over the ring
\mathbb{F}[U,V]
(see for example
[
Zem19b
]
). In the knot Floer complex over
\mathbb{F}[U,V]
, there is a bigrading
\gr=(\gr_{U},\gr_{V})
assigned to each generator, where
\gr(U)=(-2,0)
and
\gr(V)=(-2,0)
. Two knot Floer complexes are
locally equivalent
over
\mathbb{F}[U,V]
if the two knots are (smoothly) concordant (
[
Zem19a
, Section 2.3]
). The local equivalence takes on slightly different meanings when defined over different rings, but is defined to always respect the knot concordance. We are generally interested in, within different contexts, the morphism between the monoid of knots over concordance and the set of knot Floer complexes over the local equivalence relation.

No matter over which ring it is defined, the knot Floer complex always possesses certain symmetric property. For example, over the ring
\mathbb{F}[U,V]
this entails that after exchanging the role of
U
and
V
, the resulting complex is homotopy equivalent to the original knot Floer complex.

In
[
DHST21b
]
, Dai-Hom-Stoffregen-Truong studied knot Floer complexes over the ring
\mathbb{F}[U,V]/(UV).
The results were initially for knots in
S^{3},
but easily generalizable to knots in integer homology sphere
L
–spaces. According to
[
DHST21b
, Definition 4.3]
, a
standard complex
over
\mathbb{F}[U,V]/(UV)
is freely generated by
\{x_{0},x_{1},\cdots,x_{2l}\}
for some
l\in\mathbb{N},
where each pair of generators
x_{2i}
and
x_{2i+1}
is connected by some “U-arrow” and each pair of generators
x_{2i+1}
and
x_{2i+2}
is connected by some “V-arrow” for
i=0,\cdots,l-1
. All information of a standard complex can be encoded using a sequence of signed integers describing the length and the direction of the arrows in order. Thus we shall use this sequence to indicate a standard complex. It turns out that the complexes over the ring
\mathbb{F}[U,V]/(UV)
are quite nice. Indeed, we have

Theorem

(Theorem 6.1 in
[
DHST21b
]
)
.

The knot Floer complex of any knot in an integer homology sphere
L
–space is locally equivalent to a unique standard complex over
\mathbb{F}[U,V]/(UV).

Therefore, the sequence of signed integers is a concordance invariant through this identification. In
[
DHST21b
, Definition 7.1]
, the invariant
\varphi_{i}
is defined to be a signed count of the times number
i
appear in the sequence in odd position, and subsequently in
[
DHST21b
, Theorem 7.2]
, is shown to be a homomorphism from the knot concordance group
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathbb{Z}
. Equivalently we can define
\varphi_{i}
by counting only in even position (inverting the sign for each integer since we want the two definitions to be consistent); either way only half of the sequence is needed due to the symmetry. The two definitions correspond respectively to counting all the “U-arrows” or “V-arrows” of a certain length.

Setting
U=0
in the ring
\mathbb{F}[U,V]/(UV)
makes the knot Floer complex a module over
\mathbb{F}[V]
; call the homology of the resulting complex the
vertical homology.
Since
\mathbb{F}[V]
is a PID, the vertical homology of any knot Floer complex has the decomposition

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}[V]\oplus\big(\bigoplus_{j}\mathbb{F}[V]/(V^{j})\big).

A basis on the knot Floer complex that realizes this decomposition is called a
vertical basis;
namely, a vertical basis consists of generators that pairwise generate the
V
–torsions and a standalone generator (i.e. in the kernel but not the image of
\partial
) that generates the single copy of
\mathbb{F}[V]
. Furthermore by setting
V=1,
the resulting complex computes
\operatorname{\widehat{HF}}(Y)\cong\mathbb{F},
where
Y
is the ambient manifold. Therefore the standalone generator in a vertical basis has
\gr_{V}=d(Y),
or we say the
d
–invariant is supported in said generator.

In
[
DHST21a
]
, the above construction is generalized by the same group of authors. They devised the following ring

\displaystyle\mathbb{X}=\frac{\mathbb{F}[U_{B},\{W_{B,i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}},V_{T},\{W_{T,i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}]}{(U_{B}V_{T},U_{B}W_{B,i}-W_{B,i+1},V_{T}W_{T,i}-W_{T,i+1})}

with a bigrading
(\gr_{1},\gr_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z},
where

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \hskip 80.00012pt\gr(U_{B})=(-2,0)&\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt&\gr(W_{B,i})=(-2i,-2)&\hskip 30.00005pt\@@ams@intertext{and}\cr\gr(V_{T})=(0,-2)&\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt&\gr(W_{T,i})=(-2,-2i)&\hskip 30.00005pt.\end{aligned}

Inside
\mathbb{X}
there are two subrings
\mathcal{R}_{U}
and
\mathcal{R}_{V}
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{U}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{F}[U_{B},\{W_{B,i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}]/(U_{B}W_{B,i}-W_{B,i+1})

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{V}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{F}[V_{T},\{W_{T,i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}]/(V_{T}W_{T,i}-W_{T,i+1}).

Note that elements in
\mathcal{R}_{U}
can be uniquely written down as
U^{i}_{B}W^{j}_{B,0},
where

\displaystyle(i,j)\in(\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0})-(\mathbb{Z}^{<0}\times\{0\}).

Namely,
i
(the power of
U_{B}
) can be negative when
j
(the power of
W_{B,0}
) is positive, but is required to be non-negative when
j=0.
This is analogous to the ring
\mathbb{F}[U,V]
localized at the maximal ideal
(U)
, denoted by
\mathbb{F}[U,U^{-1},V]
. Here
U_{B}
plays the role of
U
and
W_{B,0}
plays the role of
V
, such that we are allow to invert
U
but not
V
. However, a difference is that in
\mathcal{R}_{U}
when there is no
V
power (when
j=0
) we “remember” the power of
U
(
i
needs to be non-negative).

Similarly, the elements in
\mathcal{R}_{V}
can be written down uniquely as well. Whenever talking about the elements in
\mathcal{R}_{U}
and
\mathcal{R}_{V}
, we canonically use this expression.

The complexes over
\mathbb{F}[U,V]
can be translated to
\mathbb{X}
using the map

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle\xmapsto[\hskip 16.38895pt]{}U_{B}+W_{T,0}

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle\xmapsto[\hskip 16.38895pt]{}V_{T}+W_{B,0},

so this allows us to talk about knot Floer complexes over the ring
\mathbb{X}.

Similar to the case of
\mathbb{F}[U,V]/(UV)
, there is the notion of the standard complex over
\mathbb{X}
(
[
DHST21a
, Definition 5.1]
). A standard complex is freely generated by
\{x_{0},x_{1},\cdots,x_{2l}\}
for some
l\in\mathbb{N},
where each pair of generators
x_{2i}
and
x_{2i+1}
is connected by a differential using elements in
\mathcal{R}_{U}
and each pair of generators
x_{2i+1}
and
x_{2i+2}
is connected by a differential using elements in
\mathcal{R}_{V}
, for
i=0,\cdots,l-1
. Again, quite remarkably, we have the following.

Theorem

(Theorem 7.1 in
[
DHST21a
]
)
.

Every knot Floer complex over the ring
\mathbb{X}
is locally equivalent to a unique standard complex.

Therefore one can define the concordance invariants
\varphi_{i,j}
by either counting
\mathcal{R}_{U}
edges or
\mathcal{R}_{V}
edges in the standard complex that is locally equivalent to the given complex (
[
DHST21a
, Definition 8.1]
). For
(i,j)\in(\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0})-(\mathbb{Z}^{<0}\times\{0\})
,
\varphi_{i,j}
are indeed homomorphisms according to
[
DHST21a
, Theorem 8.2]
. When
j=0
, the map
\varphi_{i,0}
agrees with the previously defined homomorphism
\varphi_{i}
, and when
j\neq 0
, all
\varphi_{i,j}
vanish for any knot in an integer homology sphere
L
–space.

Note that for a knot Floer complex over
\mathbb{X}
, suppose we set
V_{T}=1
. This forces
\mathcal{R}_{U}=0
due to the relation
U_{B}V_{T}=0
. The resulting complex is a graded module over
\mathbb{F}[W_{T,0}]
, and once again we obtain that the
d
–invariant is supported in the non-torsion element in this complex.

We hope that the following example provides some intuition in dealing with complexes over the ring
\mathbb{X}.

x_{1}
z
y_{2}
y_{1}
x_{2}
w_{1}
w_{2}

(a)
The complex
C
in Example
8.1
.

z
y_{2}+UV^{-1}x_{2}
y_{1}
x_{2}+Uy_{1}
w_{1}
w_{2}
x_{1}+UV^{-1}x_{2}+U^{2}V^{-2}y_{1}

(b)
The complex
C
after a change of basis in
\mathbb{F}[U,V,V^{-1}]
.

Figure 8.

Example 8.1
.

Consider the following complex
C
over
\mathbb{F}[U,V]
generated by
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2},

w_{1},w_{2}
and
z
with differentials as follows (see Figure
8
)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \partial x_{1}&=U^{3}V^{2}w_{1}\hskip 40.00006pt&\partial x_{2}=U^{2}V^{4}w_{1}+U^{3}V^{3}w_{2}\\
\partial y_{1}&=U^{2}V^{3}w_{2}&\partial y_{2}=U^{3}V^{3}w_{1}+U^{4}V^{2}w_{2}\\
\partial z&=Ux_{2}+Vy_{2}.&\end{aligned}

In fact,
C
is locally equivalent to the complex
CFK(S^{3}_{1}(T_{2,11}),(T_{2,11})_{2,1}).
(This follows from Lemma
8.6
and the proof of Proposition
1.2
.) We aim to compute
\varphi_{i,j}(C)
for some suitable
i
and
j,
which necessitates studying
C
over the ring
\mathbb{X}.
To provide some intuition, we would like to first examine
C
over
\mathbb{F}[U,V]
.

Inverting
V
, in the localized ring
\mathbb{F}[U,V,V^{-1}]
, we can perform a change of basis by replacing
y_{2}
with
y_{2}+UV^{-1}x_{2}.
This simplifies the differentials:

(8.8)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \partial x_{1}&=U^{3}V^{2}w_{1}\hskip 40.00006pt&\partial x_{2}=U^{2}V^{4}w_{1}+U^{3}V^{3}w_{2}\\
\partial y_{1}&=U^{2}V^{3}w_{2}&\partial z=V(y_{2}+UV^{-1}x_{2}).\hskip 8.00003pt\end{aligned}

Observing that
x_{1},x_{2}
and
y_{1}
and their differentials make up “a connected series of line segments”, we further perform the following change of basis:

\displaystyle x_{1}

\displaystyle\xmapsto[\hskip 8.19447pt]{}x_{1}+UV^{-2}x_{2}+U^{2}V^{-2}y_{1}

\displaystyle x_{2}

\displaystyle\xmapsto[\hskip 8.19447pt]{}x_{2}+Uy_{1}.

Under this change of basis, (the image of)
y_{1}
and
w_{2}
,
x_{2}
and
w_{1}
,
z
and
y_{2}
pairwise generate the torsion in the homology while the standalone generator
x_{1}
generates the single copy of the base ring, realizing the decomposition

H_{*}(C\otimes\mathbb{F}[U,V,V^{-1}])\cong\mathbb{F}[U,V,V^{-1}]\oplus\big(\bigoplus_{j}\mathbb{F}[U,V,V^{-1}]/(U^{j})\big).

If we instead invert
U
, then one could also write down a similar change of basis over the ring
\mathbb{F}[U,U^{-1},V],
such that there is one standalone generator while all other generators appear in pairs forming the torsion in the homology. Indeed,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} y_{1}&\xmapsto[\hskip 8.19447pt]{}y_{1}+U^{-2}Vy_{2}+U^{-2}V^{2}x_{1},&\hskip 20.00003pty_{2}\xmapsto[\hskip 8.19447pt]{}y_{2}+Vy_{1},\\
x_{2}&\xmapsto[\hskip 8.19447pt]{}x_{2}+U^{-1}Vy_{2}&\end{aligned}

accomplishes the job.

Next, denote by
C_{\mathbb{X}}
the complex
C
over the ring
\mathbb{X}.
Translating using the language of ring
\mathbb{X}
, the differentials become

\displaystyle\partial x_{1}

\displaystyle=(U_{B}^{3}W_{B,0}^{2}+V_{T}^{2}W_{T,0}^{3})w_{1}

\displaystyle\partial x_{2}

\displaystyle=(U_{B}^{2}W_{B,0}^{4}+V_{T}^{4}W_{T,0}^{2})w_{1}+(U_{B}^{3}W_{B,0}^{3}+V_{T}^{3}W_{T,0}^{3})w_{2}

\displaystyle\partial y_{1}

\displaystyle=(U_{B}^{2}W_{B,0}^{3}+V_{T}^{3}W_{T,0}^{2})w_{2}

\displaystyle\partial y_{2}

\displaystyle=(U_{B}^{3}W_{B,0}^{3}+V_{T}^{3}W_{T,0}^{3})w_{1}+(U_{B}^{4}W_{B,0}^{2}+V_{T}^{2}W_{T,0}^{4})w_{2}

\displaystyle\partial z

\displaystyle=(U_{B}+W_{T,0})x_{2}+(W_{B,0}+V_{T})y_{2}.

We wish to formulate a change of basis, under which
C_{\mathbb{X}}
is a standard complex. To motivate such a change of basis, consider the following. Inside the differential of each generator, there are terms in the subring
\mathcal{R}_{V}
and terms in the subring
\mathcal{R}_{U}.
As we have seen, the ring
\mathcal{R}_{V}
is the analogue of inverting
V
inside
\mathbb{F}[U,V]
and the ring
\mathcal{R}_{U}
is the analog of inverting
U
inside
\mathbb{F}[U,V]
. If we ignore all the terms in
\mathcal{R}_{U}
and focus on the terms in
\mathcal{R}_{V}
, there clearly exists a set of basis analogous to the case of
\mathbb{F}[U,V,V^{-1}]
. Similar for
\mathcal{R}_{U}
. Thus the intuition would be to perform the two sets of the change of basis simultaneously.

First, if we set

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \hskip 25.00003pt\widetilde{x}_{2}&=x_{2}+U^{-1}_{B}W_{B,0}y_{2}\hskip 55.00008pt&\widetilde{y}_{2}&=y_{2}+V^{-1}_{T}W_{T,0}x_{2},\end{aligned}

the differentials can be simplified to

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \hskip 40.00006pt\partial x_{1}&=(U_{B}^{3}W_{B,0}^{2}+V_{T}^{2}W_{T,0}^{3})w_{1}\hskip 20.00003pt&\partial\widetilde{x}_{2}&=V_{T}^{4}W_{T,0}^{2}w_{1}+V_{T}^{3}W_{T,0}^{3}w_{2}\hskip 3.00003pt\\
\partial y_{1}&=(U_{B}^{2}W_{B,0}^{3}+V_{T}^{3}W_{T,0}^{2})w_{2}&\partial\widetilde{y}_{2}&=U_{B}^{3}W_{B,0}^{3}w_{1}+U_{B}^{4}W_{B,0}^{2}w_{2}\\
\partial z&=U_{B}\widetilde{x}_{2}+V_{T}\widetilde{y}_{2}.\end{aligned}

Compare the
\mathcal{R}_{V}
terms in the differential of each generator with the differentials in (
8.8
) and notice the similarity. With this observation in mind, we further perform the following change of basis

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \hskip 60.00009pt\widehat{x}_{1}&=x_{1}+V_{T}^{-2}W_{T,0}\widetilde{x}_{2}+V_{T}^{-2}W^{2}_{T,0}y_{1}\hskip 10.00002pt&\widehat{x}_{2}&=\widetilde{x}_{2}+W_{T,0}y_{1}\hskip 3.00003pt\\
\widehat{y}_{1}&=y_{1}+U_{B}^{-2}W_{B,0}\widetilde{y}_{2}+U_{B}^{-2}W^{2}_{B,0}x_{1}&\widehat{y}_{2}&=\widetilde{y}_{2}+W_{B,0}x_{1},\@@ams@intertext{making the differentials become}\cr\partial\widehat{x}_{1}&=U_{B}^{3}W_{B,0}^{2}w_{1}&\partial\widehat{x}_{2}&=V_{T}^{4}W_{T,0}^{2}w_{1}\\
\partial\widehat{y}_{1}&=V_{T}^{3}W_{T,0}^{2}w_{2}&\partial\widetilde{y}_{2}&=U_{B}^{4}W_{B,0}^{2}w_{2}\\
\partial z&=U_{B}\widehat{x}_{2}+V_{T}\widehat{y}_{2}.\end{aligned}

We conclude that
C_{\mathbb{X}}
under this final basis is a standard complex given by

(-(3,2),(4,2),(1,0),-(1,0),-(4,2),(3,2)).

In particular, we have

\displaystyle\varphi_{i,j}(C)=\begin{cases}-1\quad&\text{if}\hskip 5.0pt(i,j)=(3,2)\text{ or }(4,2)\\
1&\text{if}\hskip 5.0pt(i,j)=(1,0)\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

In the following subsection, we will perform most of the computations (especially when we are invoking the filtered mapping cone formula) over the original ring
\mathbb{F}[U,U^{-1}].
Only after collecting enough information and ready to compute the concordance invariants, we then move on to either the ring
\mathbb{F}[U,V]/(UV)
or
\mathbb{X}
depending on the case at hand. We will always specify the ring we are working on whenever the base ring is not
\mathbb{F}[U,U^{-1}]
or
\mathbb{F}[U]
. It is our hope that the current approach causes minimal confusion.

8.2.
Examples and applications

We begin by describing a family of concrete examples by applying the filtered mapping formula to the
+1
-surgery on the torus knot
T_{2,3}
with varying
n
.

Using the notion of the standard complex from either
[

]
or
[

]
, we have

Proposition 8.2
.

The complex
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(S^{3}_{1}(T_{2,3}),(T_{2,3})_{n,1})
is locally equivalent to the standard complex
(-1,n,1,-1,-2,\cdots,n-i+1,1,-1,-i-1,\cdots,2,1,-1,-n,1)
for some fixed
n\geq 1
, where
i=1,\cdots,n-1.

The standard complex described in the proposition is simply the concatenation of
(n-i+1,1,-1,-i-1)
for
i=1,\cdots,n-1
in that order, with
-1
and
1
in the front and back.

A_{s}
\alpha_{s}
x_{s}
y_{s}

Proof.

The case of
n=1
follows from a simple calculation. Now consider when
n>1.

Let us denote the generators of
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(S^{3},T_{2,3})
by
a,b
and
c
with coordinates
(0,0),(0,1)
and
(0,-1)
respectively, where
b
has Maslov grading
0
. They satisfy

\partial a=Ub+c.

Via the isomorphism with
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(S^{3},T_{2,3})
, we denote the generators in
A_{s}
(resp.
B_{s}
) by
a_{s},b_{s}
and
c_{s}
(resp.
a^{\prime}_{s},b^{\prime}_{s}
and
c^{\prime}_{s}
) for
0\leq s\leq n
. We will abuse the notation and use
\partial
for the differential in
X^{\infty}_{n}(T_{2,3})
from now on. For each
s=1,\cdots,n-1
, We have

\displaystyle\partial a_{s}

\displaystyle=Ub_{s}+c_{s}+a^{\prime}_{i}+U^{s}a^{\prime}_{s+1}\qquad\qquad\hbox{}

\displaystyle\partial b_{s}

\displaystyle=b^{\prime}_{s}+U^{s-1}c^{\prime}_{s+1}

\displaystyle\partial c_{s}

\displaystyle=c^{\prime}_{s}+U^{s+1}b^{\prime}_{s+1}

\displaystyle\partial a^{\prime}_{s}

\displaystyle=Ub^{\prime}_{s}+c^{\prime}_{s}.

We will establish a set of basis for the reduced complex of
X^{\infty}_{n}(T_{2,3})
. First we can let
c^{\prime}_{s}+Ub^{\prime}_{s}
replace
c^{\prime}_{s}
and quotienting out the acyclic summand generated by
a^{\prime}_{s}
and
c^{\prime}_{s}+Ub^{\prime}_{s}
. Then considering the first three relations, it is natural to perform the following change of basis (for all
0\leq s\leq n
):

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \alpha_{s}&=a_{s}\qquad\qquad&x_{s}&=b_{s}+U^{s-1}a^{\prime}_{s+1}\\
\beta_{s}&=c^{\prime}_{s}\qquad\qquad&y_{s}&=c^{\prime}_{s}+a_{s},\end{aligned}

such that under the new basis, the relations are simplified. See Figure
9
. For each
s=1,\cdots,n-1
,

\displaystyle\partial\alpha_{s}

\displaystyle=Ux_{s}+y_{s}\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\hbox{}

\displaystyle\partial x_{s}

\displaystyle=\beta_{s}+U^{s}\beta_{s+1}

\displaystyle\partial y_{s}

\displaystyle=U\beta_{s}+U^{s+1}\beta_{s+1}.

When considered as a generator of the complex
B_{s}\{i=0\},

\beta_{s}
is the unique generator of homology under the chosen basis. The
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})
filtrations of the generators can be computed using (
8.3
),(
8.4
),(
8.5
) and (
8.6
). All
\alpha_{s},\beta_{s},x_{s}
and

and their

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(\alpha_{s})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}n^{2}+\frac{1}{2}n+ns-s

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(\beta_{s})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}n^{2}+\frac{1}{2}n+n(s-1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(x_{s})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}n^{2}+\frac{1}{2}n+ns-(s-1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(y_{s})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}n^{2}+\frac{1}{2}n+ns-(s+1).

The differential of
\alpha_{s}
consists of
Ux_{s}
and
y_{s}
, which differ by
(1,-1)
in the
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})

and
y_{s}
. The
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})

(0,n-s+1)
from

to

and equal to
(s,1)
from

to

. Note that
\partial y_{s}=\partial Ux_{s}
, from this immediately follows that the

grading shifts in the differential of

are
(1,n-s)
and
(s+1,0)
respectively.

We then consider the generators in
A_{0}
. After performing a change of basis

\displaystyle x_{0}

\displaystyle\xmapsto{}x_{0}+U^{-1}y_{0}

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}

\displaystyle\xmapsto{}\alpha_{0},

and quotienting out the resulting acyclic summand, the only remaining generator in
A_{0}
is
y_{0}
, with the differential given by

\partial y_{0}=U\beta_{1}.

The
\mathcal{J}
filtrations of
y_{0}
and
U\beta_{1}
are equal.

With the above computations ready, the rest of the proof assesses the complex over the ring
\mathbb{F}[U,V]/(UV)
, using the language of
[
DHST21b
]
. The sub-quotient complex generated by
\alpha_{s},\beta_{s}x_{s}
and
y_{s}
corresponds to the sequence
(n-s+1,1,-1,-s-1)
, starting from
\beta_{s}
. See Figure
10
. Next observe that each
\beta_{s}
is in the image of
x_{s}
and
y_{s+1}
(with some appropriate
U,V
decoration), thus as

ranges from
1
to
n-1
, the sub-quoitent complex generated by all
\alpha_{s},\beta_{s},x_{s}
and
y_{s}
corresponds to the concatenation of
(n-s+1,1,-1,-s-1)
.

Finally, it is easy to see that by setting
U=0
, each pair of generators
\alpha_{s}
and
y_{s}
,
\beta_{s}
and
x_{s}
for
n\geq s\geq 1
makes up a torsion summand in the vertical homology while
y_{0}
generates the single copy of
\mathbb{F}[V]
. Therefore by further setting
V=1
,
\operatorname{\widehat{HF}}(S^{3}_{1}(T_{2,3}))
(and thus the
d
-invariant) is supported in
y_{0}
as required. The differential of
y_{0}
gives the
-1
in the beginning of the sequence. Following from the symmetry, we obtain the
1
at the end of the sequence.
∎

Remark 8.3
.

In fact, the proof shows that over the ring
\mathbb{F}[U,V],

\operatorname{CFK}(S^{3},T_{2,3})/(UV)
is isomorphic to the standard complex
(-1,\cdots,n-i+1,1,-1,-i-1,\cdots,1)
, with
i=1,\cdots,n-1.
Furthermore, this information completely determines
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(S^{3},T_{2,3})
(via the condition
\partial^{2}=0
).

We are ready to prove the computational result Proposition
1.2
which leads to Theorem
1.1
.
Recall that torus knots
T_{2,4k+3}
are
L
–space knots with genus
g=2k+1
, where
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. (We have so far reserved the letter
k
for the surgery coefficient. Since in this section the surgery is mostly fixed, hopefully this new assignment of letter
k
does not cause confusion.) The knot Floer complex
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(S^{3},T_{2,4k+3})
is generated by
a_{i}
for
i=1,\cdots,g
with coordinate
(0,-g+2i-1)
and
b_{i}
for
i=1,\cdots,g+1
with coordinate
(0,-g+2i-2)
. The Maslov grading is supported in
b_{g+1}
and the differentials are given by

\partial a_{i}=Ub_{i+1}+b_{i},\quad\text{for}\hskip 5.0pti=1,\cdots,g.

As before, let
(T_{2,4k+3})_{n,1}
denote the
(n,1)
–cable of the meridian inside
+1
-surgery on
T_{2,4k+3}
and let
J_{n,k}
denote
(T_{2,4k+3})_{n,1}
connected sum with the unknot in
-S^{3}_{1}(T_{2,4k+3})
. The ambient manifold
S^{3}_{1}(T_{2,4k+3})\mathbin{\#}-S^{3}_{1}(T_{2,4k+3})
is homology cobordant to
S^{3}
.

See
1.2

This immediately implies Theorem
1.1
. In fact, we can prove a stronger version.

Theorem 8.4
.

For any integers
i
and
j
such that
i>j\geq 0,
and a given sequence of arbitrary integers
(h_{j+1},\cdots,h_{i})
,
there exists some knot
K\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
\varphi_{t,j}(K)=h_{t}
for
t=j+1,\cdots,i,
and
\varphi_{t,j}(K)=0
for
t>i.

Proof.

The concordance invariant
\varphi_{i,j}
is additive under the group action of
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. By Proposition
1.2
, we can first choose a knot with
\varphi_{i,j}=h_{i}
. Assuming
i-j>1,
then by adding copies of
J_{i-1,j}
to the chosen knot, we can make it such that
\varphi_{i-1,j}=h_{i-1}
while the value of
\varphi_{i,j}
remains unchanged. Repeat this process until a knot with desired property is obtained.
∎

\displaystyle\partial(a_{i})_{s}

\displaystyle=(b_{i})_{s}+U(b_{i+1})_{s}+(a_{i})^{\prime}_{s}+U^{s+g+1-2i}(a_{g-i+1})^{\prime}_{s+1}

We first aim to choose a reduced basis for the complex of

i\geq\frac{s+g+2}{2}&\quad\text{or}\quad i\leq\frac{s+g}{2}-\left\lceil\frac{n-1}{2}\right\rceil&\text{ if }s\text{ is odd},\end{aligned}

and when

, if and only if

i\leq\frac{g+1}{2}-\left\lceil\frac{n-s}{2}\right\rceil.

We will show the claim when

and

In order to simplify the notation when talking about the filtration, define

(8.11)

f(n,s)=-\frac{(n-1)n}{2}+ns,

so that the

When

if and only if

In either case, the second half of the claim is verified. Thus we have obtained a reduced basis for the complex
X^{\infty}_{n}(T_{2,4k+3})
as follows. The generators in
A^{\infty}_{s}
are given by
(\alpha_{i})_{s},(x_{i})_{s}
for

, such that when

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \frac{s+g+1}{2}\geq i\geq\text{max}\{1,\frac{s+g+1}{2}-\left\lfloor\frac{n-1}{2}\right\rfloor\}&\text{ if }s\text{ is even,}\\
\frac{s+g+2}{2}\geq i\geq\text{max}\{1,\frac{s+g+2}{2}-\left\lceil\frac{n-1}{2}\right\rceil\}&\text{ if }s\text{ is odd},\end{aligned}

when
s\geq g
,

g+1\geq i\geq\text{max}\{1,\frac{g+3}{2}-\left\lceil\frac{n-s}{2}\right\rceil\};

the generators in

are

for

and the differentials are given by (

) and (

).

Before moving on, let us introduce some notational shorthands:
denote by
x_{s}
the “top” generator in each
A_{s}
complex, i.e. the one with the largest
i
index; for example, when
-g+1\leq s\leq g
and
s
is even,
x_{s}=(x_{(s+g+1)/2})_{s}
. Let
i^{(t)}_{n,s}
be this “top”
i
index. Similarly, let
i^{(b)}_{n,s}
be the smallest integer that
i
index can take in each case. Here letters
t
and
b
are chosen to indicate “top” and “bottom” respectively.

We want to take a look at the
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})
filtration shifts next. Clearly, the filtration shifts in the differential of each
(\alpha_{i})_{s}
are
(1,0)
and
(0,1).
So we focus on the filtration shifts in the differential of each
(x_{i})_{s}
. Suppose
U^{c}\beta_{s^{\prime}}
is a nontrivial term in
\partial(x_{i})_{s}
, where
s^{\prime}=s
or
s+1.
Define

(8.12)

\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}((x_{i})_{s},\beta_{s^{\prime}})=(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})((x_{i})_{s})-(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})(U^{c}\beta_{s^{\prime}})

and similarly define
\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}
and
\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}
. Note that we have

(8.13)

\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}((x_{i-1})_{s},\beta_{s^{\prime}})=\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}((x_{i})_{s},\beta_{s^{\prime}})+(1,-1)

for
i^{(t)}_{n,s}\geq i\geq i^{(b)}_{n,s}+1
, therefore to completely understand the filtration shifts of all the generators in each complex
A_{s}
, we need only compute the filtration shift of the “top” generator
x_{s}
.

Lemma 8.5
.

When
-g+1\leq s\leq g,
if
s
is even,

(8.14)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(x_{s},\beta_{s})

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{-s+g+1}{2},n+\frac{-s+g-1}{2}\Big)

(8.15)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(x_{s},\beta_{s+1})

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{s+g+1}{2},\frac{s+g-1}{2}\Big);

if
s
is odd,

(8.16)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(x_{s},\beta_{s})

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{-s+g}{2},n+\frac{-s+g}{2}\Big)

(8.17)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(x_{s},\beta_{s+1})

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{s+g}{2},\frac{s+g}{2}\Big);

when
g\leq s\leq g+n-1
,

(8.18)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(x_{s},\beta_{s})

\displaystyle=(0,n+g-s)

(8.19)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(x_{s},\beta_{s+1})

\displaystyle=(s,g).

Proof.

For each case,
\mathcal{J}(\beta_{s})=f(n,s)-n
and
\mathcal{J}(\beta_{s+1})=f(n,s)
by (
8.6
). When

-g+1\leq s\leq g,
and
s
is even,

\displaystyle\partial x_{s}

\displaystyle=U^{(-s+g+1)/2}\beta_{s}+U^{(s+g+1)/2}\beta_{s+1},

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(x_{s})

\displaystyle=f(n,s)-1.

Compute

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(x_{s},\beta_{s})

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{-s+g+1}{2},\frac{-s+g+1}{2}\Big)+(0,n-1)

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{-s+g+1}{2},n+\frac{-s+g-1}{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(x_{s},\beta_{s+1})

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{s+g+1}{2},\frac{s+g+1}{2}\Big)+(0,-1)

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{s+g+1}{2},\frac{s+g-1}{2}\Big).

When
-g+1\leq s\leq g,
and
s
is odd, the computation is parallel. Suppose now
g\leq s\leq g+n-1
, then we have

\displaystyle\partial x_{s}

\displaystyle=\beta_{s}+U^{s}\beta_{s+1},

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(x_{s})

\displaystyle=f(n,s)-(s-g).

So we may compute

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(x_{s},\beta_{s})

\displaystyle=(0,n+g-s)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(x_{s},\beta_{s+1})

\displaystyle=(s,s)+(0,g-s)

\displaystyle=(s,g).

∎

Next we claim that for the purpose of computing concordance invariants, we need only look at a portion of the mapping cone.
Define
X^{\infty}_{n}(K)\langle l\rangle=X^{\infty}_{1,n,-l+n,l}(K)
for
l\in\mathbb{Z}
, which is the filtered mapping cone

\displaystyle\bigoplus^{l}_{s=-l+n}A^{\infty}_{s}\xrightarrow{v^{\infty}_{s}+h^{\infty}_{s}}\bigoplus^{l}_{s=-l+n+1}B^{\infty}_{s}.

Note that under this notation
X^{\infty}_{n}(K)=X^{\infty}_{n}(K)\langle g+n-1\rangle
.

Lemma 8.6
.

For any
k\geq 1
and
n\geq 1
, the filtered complex
X^{\infty}_{n}(T_{2,4k+3})\langle g+n-1\rangle
is isomorphic to
X^{\infty}_{n}(T_{2,4k+3})\langle n\rangle\oplus D
up to a change of basis, where
H_{*}(D)=0
.

Proof.

It suffices to show for each
l=n+1,\cdots,g+n-1,
the complex
X^{\infty}_{n}(T_{2,4k+3})\langle l\rangle
is isomorphic to
X^{\infty}_{n}(T_{2,4s+3})\langle l-1\rangle\oplus D
up to a change of basis, where
H_{*}(D)=0
. For every such
l
, we will demonstrate a filtered change of basis such that the complex given by

(8.20)

A^{\infty}_{-l+n}\xrightarrow{h_{-l+n}}B^{\infty}_{-l+n+1}

becomes a summand under the new basis. Thus following from the symmetry, there is also a filtered change of basis such that

(8.21)

A^{\infty}_{l}\xrightarrow{\hskip 2.45836ptv_{l}\hskip 2.45836pt}B^{\infty}_{l}

becomes a summand under the new basis as required.

Let
s=-l+n+1
in the following proof, and we have
s\leq 0.
There are two cases to consider depending on the parity of
s.

•

When
s
is even, perform the change of basis

\displaystyle(x_{i})_{s}\xmapsto[\hskip 16.38895pt\hbox{}]{}

\displaystyle(x_{i})_{s}+U^{\frac{-s+g+3}{2}-i}x_{{s}-1},\quad\text{for}\hskip 5.0pti^{(t)}_{n,s}\geq i\geq i^{(b)}_{n,s}.

By (
8.9
) and (
8.10
) it is straightforward to check that the complex given by (
8.20
) is a summand under this basis. (Note that in complex
X^{\infty}_{n}(T_{2,4k+3})\langle l\rangle
the map
v_{-l+n}=v_{s-1}
is the zero map.) It remains to show that this change of basis is filtered. This in fact amounts to showing for
i^{(t)}_{n,s}\geq i\geq i^{(b)}_{n,s}
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}((x_{i})_{s},\beta_{s})\geq\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(x_{s-1},\beta_{s}).

Thus we compute

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}

\displaystyle((x_{i})_{s},\beta_{s})-\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(x_{s-1},\beta_{s})

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(x_{s},\beta_{s})+\left(\frac{s+g+1}{2}-i\right)(1,-1)-\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(x_{s-1},\beta_{s})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{-s+g+1}{2},n+\frac{-s+g-1}{2}\right)+\left(\frac{s+g+1}{2}-i,-\frac{s+g+1}{2}+i\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt-\left(\frac{s-1+g}{2},\frac{s-1+g}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{-s+g+3}{2}-i,n-1+\frac{-3s-g+1}{2}+i\right).

The
\mathcal{I}
filtration shift is clearly positive for all
i
since

\displaystyle\frac{-s+g+3}{2}-i

\displaystyle\geq\frac{-s+g+3}{2}-\frac{s+g+1}{2}

\displaystyle=-s+1

\displaystyle>0,

while for the
\mathcal{J}
filtration shift there are two possible cases depending on the value of
i^{(b)}_{n,s}
:

i)

Suppose

1\geq\frac{s+g+1}{2}-\left\lfloor\frac{n-1}{2}\right\rfloor,

then we have

\displaystyle n-1+\frac{-3s-g+1}{2}+i

\displaystyle\geq n+\frac{-3s-g+1}{2}

\displaystyle\geq 2\left\lfloor\frac{n-1}{2}\right\rfloor+1+\frac{-3s-g+1}{2}

\displaystyle\geq s+g+\frac{-3s-g+1}{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{-s+g+1}{2}

\displaystyle>0.

ii)

If instead

1\leq\frac{s+g+1}{2}-\left\lfloor\frac{n-1}{2}\right\rfloor,

this is when
n
is relatively small, and we have

\displaystyle n-1+\frac{-3s-g+1}{2}+i

\displaystyle\geq n-1+\frac{-3s-g+1}{2}+\frac{s+g+1}{2}-\left\lfloor\frac{n-1}{2}\right\rfloor

\displaystyle\geq-s+1-\left\lfloor\frac{n-1}{2}\right\rfloor+n-1

\displaystyle\geq-s+1+\frac{n-1}{2}

\displaystyle>0.

•

When
s
is odd, perform the change of basis

\displaystyle(x_{i})_{s}\xmapsto[\hskip 16.38895pt\hbox{}]{}

\displaystyle(x_{i})_{s}+U^{\frac{-s+g+2}{2}-i}x_{s-1},\quad\text{for}\hskip 5.0pti^{(t)}_{n,s}\geq i\geq i^{(b)}_{n,s}.

Again one can verify under this basis the complex given by (
8.20
) is a summand. The difference in
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})
filtration is

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}

\displaystyle((x_{i})_{s},\beta_{s})-\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(x_{s-1},\beta_{s})

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(x_{s},\beta_{s})+\left(\frac{s+g+2}{2}-i\right)(1,-1)-\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(x_{s-1},\beta_{s})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{-s+g}{2},n+\frac{-s+g}{2}\right)+\left(\frac{s+g+2}{2}-i,-\frac{s+g+2}{2}+i\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt-\left(\frac{s+g}{2},\frac{s+g-2}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{-s+g+2}{2}-i,n+\frac{-3s-g}{2}+i\right).

The
\mathcal{I}
filtration shift is clearly positive since

\displaystyle\frac{-s+g+2}{2}-i

\displaystyle\geq\frac{-s+g+2}{2}-\frac{s+g+2}{2}

\displaystyle=-s

\displaystyle>0,

while the
\mathcal{J}
filtration has two possible cases depending on the value of
i^{(b)}_{n,s}
.

i)

Suppose that

1\geq\frac{s+g+2}{2}-\left\lceil\frac{n-1}{2}\right\rceil,

then we have

\displaystyle n+\frac{-3s-g}{2}+i

\displaystyle\geq n+\frac{-3s-g}{2}+1

\displaystyle\geq 2\left\lceil\frac{n-1}{2}\right\rceil+\frac{-3s-g}{2}+1

\displaystyle\geq s+g+\frac{-3s-g}{2}+1

\displaystyle\geq\frac{-s+g}{2}+1

\displaystyle>0.

ii)

If instead

1\leq\frac{s+g+1}{2}-\left\lfloor\frac{n-1}{2}\right\rfloor,

in this case we have

\displaystyle n+\frac{-3s-g}{2}+i

\displaystyle\geq n+\frac{-3s-g}{2}+\frac{s+g+2}{2}-\left\lceil\frac{n-1}{2}\right\rceil

\displaystyle\geq-s+1+\left\lceil\frac{n-1}{2}\right\rceil

\displaystyle>0

as required.

∎

Proof of Proposition
1.2
.

By Lemma
8.6
, in order to compute concordance invariants it suffices to consider the complex
X^{\infty}_{n}(T_{2,4k+3})\langle n\rangle
. We claim that over the ring
\mathbb{X}
discussed in the previous subsection
8.1
, after a change of basis
X^{\infty}_{n}(T_{2,4k+3})\langle n\rangle
is a standard complex.

Translating to the ring
\mathbb{X}
, the differential on the complex is given by

\displaystyle\partial(\alpha_{i})_{s}

\displaystyle=(U_{B}+W_{T,0})(x_{i+1})_{s}+(W_{B,0}+V_{T})(x_{i})_{s}

\displaystyle\partial{{(x_{i})}}_{0}

\displaystyle=\big(U^{\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}({(x_{i})}_{0},\beta_{1})}_{B}W^{\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}({(x_{i})}_{0},\beta_{1})}_{B,0}+V^{\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}({(x_{i})}_{0},\beta_{1})}_{T}W^{\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}({(x_{i})}_{0},\beta_{s+1})}_{T,0}\big)\beta_{1}

\displaystyle\partial{{(x_{i})}}_{n}

\displaystyle=\big(U^{\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}({(x_{i})}_{n},\beta_{n})}_{B}W^{\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}({(x_{i})}_{n},\beta_{n})}_{B,0}+V^{\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}({(x_{i})}_{n},\beta_{n})}_{T}W^{\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}({(x_{i})}_{n},\beta_{n})}_{T,0}\big)\beta_{n}

and if
n>s>0,
then

\displaystyle\partial{{(x_{i})}}_{s}

\displaystyle=\big(U^{\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}({(x_{i})}_{s},\beta_{s})}_{B}W^{\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}({(x_{i})}_{s},\beta_{s})}_{B,0}+V^{\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}({(x_{i})}_{s},\beta_{s})}_{T}W^{\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}({(x_{i})}_{s},\beta_{s})}_{T,0}\big)\beta_{s}

\displaystyle+\big(

\displaystyle U^{\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}({(x_{i})}_{s},\beta_{s+1})}_{B}W^{\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}({(x_{i})}_{s},\beta_{s+1})}_{B,0}+V^{\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}({(x_{i})}_{s},\beta_{s+1})}_{T}W^{\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}({(x_{i})}_{s},\beta_{s+1})}_{T,0}\big)\beta_{s+1}.

We first perform the change of basis

\displaystyle(\widetilde{x}_{i})_{s}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(x_{i})_{s}+U^{-1}_{B}W_{B,0}(x_{i-1})_{s}&\quad\text{if}\hskip 5.0pti=i^{(t)}_{n,s}\\
(x_{i})_{s}+U^{-1}_{B}W_{B,0}(x_{i-1})_{s}+V^{-1}_{T}W_{T,0}(x_{i+1})_{s}&\quad\text{if}\hskip 5.0pti^{(b)}_{n,s}<i<i^{(t)}_{n,s}\\
(x_{i})_{s}+V^{-1}_{T}W_{T,0}(x_{i+1})_{s}&\quad\text{if}\hskip 5.0pti=i^{(b)}_{n,s},\end{cases}

which simplifies the differentials considerably:

\displaystyle\partial(\alpha_{i})_{s}

\displaystyle=U_{B}(\widetilde{x}_{i+1})_{s}+V_{T}(\widetilde{x}_{i})_{s}

\displaystyle\partial{(\widetilde{x}_{i})}_{0}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}V^{\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}({(x_{i})}_{0},\beta_{1})}_{T}W^{\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}({(x_{i})}_{0},\beta_{1})}_{T,0}\beta_{1},\hskip 5.0pti=i^{(t)}_{n,0}\\
U^{\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}({(x_{i})}_{0},\beta_{1})}_{B}W^{\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}({(x_{i})}_{0},\beta_{1})}_{B,0}\beta_{1},\hskip 5.0pti=i^{(b)}_{n,0}\\
0,\hskip 100.00015pt\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\partial{(\widetilde{x}_{i})}_{n}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}V^{\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}({(x_{i})}_{n},\beta_{n})}_{T}W^{\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}({(x_{i})}_{n},\beta_{n})}_{T,0}\beta_{n},\hskip 5.0pti=i^{(t)}_{n,n}\\
U^{\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}({(x_{i})}_{n},\beta_{n})}_{B}W^{\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}({(x_{i})}_{n},\beta_{n})}_{B,0}\beta_{n},\hskip 5.0pti=i^{(b)}_{n,n}\\
0,\hskip 104.00009pt\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

and for
n>s>0,

\displaystyle\partial{(\widetilde{x}_{i})}_{s}=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}V^{\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}({(x_{i})}_{s},\beta_{s})}_{T}W^{\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}({(x_{i})}_{s},\beta_{s})}_{T,0}\beta_{s}+V^{\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}({(x_{i})}_{s},\beta_{s+1})}_{T}W^{\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}({(x_{i})}_{s},\beta_{s+1})}_{T,0}\beta_{s+1},\hskip 5.0pti=i^{(t)}_{n,s}\\
U^{\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}({(x_{i})}_{s},\beta_{s})}_{B}W^{\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}({(x_{i})}_{s},\beta_{s})}_{B,0}\beta_{s}+U^{\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}({(x_{i})}_{s},\beta_{s+1})}_{B}W^{\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}({(x_{i})}_{s},\beta_{s+1})}_{B,0}\beta_{s+1},\hskip 5.0pti=i^{(b)}_{n,s}\\
0,\hskip 245.00037pt\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Next observe that

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}(x_{s},\beta_{s+1})-\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}(x_{s+1},\beta_{s+1})=\begin{cases}s+1\quad&\text{if}\hskip 5.0pt0\leq s\leq g\\
s&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

by (
8.14
), (
8.15
), (
8.16
), (
8.17
), (
8.18
) and (
8.19
). In particular, for any
s
with
0\leq s\leq n-1,
we have
\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}(x_{s},\beta_{s+1})-\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}(x_{s+1},\beta_{s+1})>0.
Similarly, one can show
\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}((x_{i^{(b)}_{n,s}})_{s},\beta_{s})-\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}((x_{i^{(b)}_{n,s-1}})_{s-1},\beta_{s})>0
for
n\geq s\geq 1
.

Therefore we may further perform a change of basis

(8.22)

\displaystyle(\widetilde{x}_{i^{(t)}_{n,s}})_{s}

\displaystyle\xmapsto[\hskip 16.38895pt]{}\sum_{n\geq s^{\prime}\geq s}V^{\lambda_{s^{\prime}}}_{T}W^{\mu_{s^{\prime}}}_{T,0}(\widetilde{x}_{i^{(t)}_{n,s^{\prime}}})_{s^{\prime}}\quad\text{for all}\hskip 5.0ptn>s\geq 0,

(8.23)

\displaystyle(\widetilde{x}_{i^{(b)}_{n,s}})_{s}

\displaystyle\xmapsto[\hskip 16.38895pt]{}\sum_{0\leq s^{\prime}\leq s}U^{\theta_{s^{\prime}}}_{B}W^{\eta_{s^{\prime}}}_{B,0}(\widetilde{x}_{i^{(b)}_{n,s^{\prime}}})_{s^{\prime}}\quad\text{for all}\hskip 5.0pt0<s\leq n,

where

\displaystyle\lambda_{s^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s^{\prime}-1\geq t\geq s}\big(\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}(x_{t},\beta_{t+1})-\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}(x_{t+1},\beta_{t+1})\big)

\displaystyle\mu_{s^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s^{\prime}-1\geq t\geq s}\big(\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}(x_{t},\beta_{t+1})-\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}(x_{t+1},\beta_{t+1})\big)\hskip 50.00008pt,

and
\theta_{s^{\prime}},\eta_{s^{\prime}}
can be defined similarly. Note that the definition implies
\lambda_{s}=\mu_{s}=0.
When
s^{\prime}>s
(resp.
s^{\prime}<s
),
\mu_{s^{\prime}}
(resp.
\eta_{s^{\prime}}
) is positive, while
\lambda_{s^{\prime}}
(resp.
\theta_{s^{\prime}}
) needs not to be (in fact they are negative). This change of basis results in a standard complex as required.

Let
(\widetilde{x})_{s}
denote the image of
(\widetilde{x}_{i^{(t)}_{n,s}})_{s}
under the above change of basis. Then we have
(\widetilde{x})_{0}\in\operatorname{ker}\partial
, and starting from
(\widetilde{x})_{0}
every odd number of edge is marked by elements in
\mathcal{R}_{U}
and every even number of edge is marked by elements in
\mathcal{R}_{V}
. In order to compute
\varphi_{i,j},
we need only consider the set of (say)
\mathcal{R}_{V}
edges. Each
\mathcal{R}_{V}
edge that is not equal to
V_{T}
is marked by the differential of
(\widetilde{x})_{s}
for some
s=1,\cdots,n.
For
1\leq s\leq n,

\displaystyle\partial(\widetilde{x})_{s}=V^{\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}(x_{s},\beta_{s})}_{T}W^{\Delta_{\mathcal{I}}(x_{s},\beta_{s})}_{T,0}\beta_{s}

thus we simply need to look at the values of
\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(x_{s},\beta_{s}).

For the knot
T_{2,4k+3}
,
g=2k+1.
By (
8.14
), (
8.16
) and (
8.18
), when
n\geq g
, the sequence of
\Delta_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(x_{s},\beta_{s})
for
s=1,\cdots,n
is given by

\displaystyle(k,n+k),(k,n+k-1),\cdots,(1,n+1),(1,n),(0,n),(0,n-1),\cdots,(0,2k+1);

when
n\leq g
, the above sequence terminates at
(k+1-n/2,n/2+k)
if
n
is even and at
(k-(n-1)/2,(n+1)/2+k)
if
n
is odd.

Therefore we conclude that for the interested complex, the concordance invariant
\varphi_{i,j}=-1
if and only if
(i,j)=(n+k,k),(n+k-1,k),\cdots
and
\varphi_{i,j}=0
if
(i,j)
is not among those values and also
(i,j)\neq(1,0).

∎

We now prove Proposition
1.7
with a more precise restatement. The result is stated for knots in
S^{3}
, but a similar result should hold for knots in rational homology spheres as well.

Proposition 8.7
.

For a knot
K\subset S^{3},
when
n\geq 2g,
as a quotient complex of the filtered mapping cone (for any surgery),
A_{\left\lceil n/2\right\rceil}
is filtered homotopy equivalent to
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(S^{3},K).

Proof.

When
n\geq 2g,

\left\lceil n/2\right\rceil\geq g.
Note that all the generators of
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(S^{3},K)
are within the region of
-g\leq i+j\leq g.
It is then straightforward to check using (
8.3
) and (
8.4
) that this region is filtered.
∎

Finally let us analyze the behavior of the complex
X^{\infty}_{n}(K)
when
n\geq 2g.
Comparing
X^{\infty}_{n}(K)
and
X^{\infty}_{n+1}(K)
, we can identify
A_{s}
in both complex for
s\leq\left\lfloor n/2\right\rfloor.
Under this identification, one can check that
\mathcal{J}(y_{s})-\mathcal{J}(h_{s}(y_{s}))
is constant while
\mathcal{J}(y_{s})-\mathcal{J}(v_{s}(y_{s}))
increases as
n
increases, and the
\mathcal{I}
filtration shift is constant. At the same time, we can identify
A_{s}\subset X^{\infty}_{n}(K)
with
A_{s+1}\subset X^{\infty}_{n+1}(K)
for
s\geq\left\lfloor n/2\right\rfloor+1>g
, and under this identification, similarly the filtration shifts from the
v
map is constant while the filtration shifts from the
h
map increases as
n
increases. Furthermore, by Proposition
8.7
, the “middle” complex
A_{\left\lceil(n+1)/2\right\rceil}
is simply a copy of
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(S^{3},K)
(with some differentials pointing to the rest of the complex).

Therefore, when
n\geq 2g
and as
n
increases by
1
, we conclude that there are two things happen to
X^{\infty}_{n}(K)
:

•

certain edges are extended;

•

a copy of
\operatorname{CFK}^{\infty}(S^{3},K)
is added to the “middle” of the complex.

Aside from these two changes, the complex remains the same “shape” as
n
increases. We name this phenomenon
stabilization
. For an example, note that the whole family described by Proposition
8.2
can be seen as the result of continued stabilizations. In general, with slightly more careful analysis, one should be able to pin down the exact behavior of a given complex during the stabilization.
